  \item \textbf{Number locale.} Like Germany, the thousands separator is a \textbf{dot} and the decimal mark
    a comma: \texttt{1.234.567,89} = 1{,}234{,}567.89 --- the \emph{opposite} of the French space. Amounts
    are usually \emph{milioni} (or \emph{migliaia}) \emph{di euro}.
  \item \textbf{Statements run across pages with ``(segue)''.} A single statement is split over consecutive
    pages marked \emph{(segue)} (``continued''): Eni's \emph{variazioni} spans three pages and its
    \emph{rendiconto} two; Enel's \emph{stato patrimoniale} and \emph{variazioni} two each. Each page becomes
    its own table row of the same class --- expected, not an error.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The account-form (\emph{T}-shaped) balance sheet}
\label{sec:esef-italy-tshape}

Italy's statutory balance sheet is \textbf{account-form}, and --- unlike the Spanish case
(\S\ref{sec:esef-spain-tshape}) --- it is defined by \textbf{law}. Under \textbf{Codice Civile art.~2424},
the \emph{Stato patrimoniale} is drawn up \emph{``a sezioni contrapposte''} --- \textbf{contrasting sections,
side by side}: the \emph{Attivo} (assets) on the left, the \emph{Passivo} (liabilities \emph{and} net equity,
\emph{patrimonio netto}) on the right. Each side is a numbered hierarchy: macro-classes in capital letters
(\emph{Attivo}: \emph{A}~crediti verso soci, \emph{B}~immobilizzazioni, \emph{C}~attivo circolante,
\emph{D}~ratei e risconti; \emph{Passivo}: \emph{A}~patrimonio netto, \emph{B}~fondi per rischi e oneri,
\emph{C}~trattamento di fine rapporto, \emph{D}~debiti, \emph{E}~ratei e risconti), then Roman-numeral
classes and Arabic-numeral items. So this is the genuine \textbf{account-form / horizontal ``T''} --- and
it is verified from the \emph{legal schema itself}, which is stronger evidence than any single filing.%
\footnote{\emph{Codice Civile} art.~2424, ``Contenuto dello stato patrimoniale''; the \emph{sezioni
contrapposte} (facing-sections) layout is confirmed across authoritative Italian sources ---
\href{https://www.informazionefiscale.it/stato-patrimoniale-schema-art-2424-bilancio}{informazionefiscale.it},
\href{https://www.laleggepertutti.it/codice-civile/art-2424-codice-civile-contenuto-dello-stato-patrimoniale}{La~Legge per Tutti},
and the standard-setter \emph{OIC} (Organismo Italiano di Contabilità),
\href{https://www.fondazioneoic.eu/wp-content/uploads/2022/10/2019-01-OIC-12-Composizione-e-schemi-del-bilancio.pdf}{OIC~12 --- Composizione e schemi del bilancio}.}

\noindent \textbf{A nuance --- the same split as everywhere in this chapter.} The account-form is the
\emph{national-GAAP} (OIC) \textbf{statutory} format, used by \textbf{unlisted} companies. \textbf{Listed}
Italian issuers prepare their financial statements under \textbf{IFRS} --- for the \emph{consolidated and the
separate (parent)} accounts alike (\emph{D.Lgs.}~38/2005) --- so their ESEF filings are \textbf{report-form
IFRS}, \emph{not} the ``T''. The account-form therefore lives on the \textbf{unlisted\,/\,PDF side} (accounts
deposited at the \emph{Registro delle Imprese}), not in the ESEF listed filings --- exactly the
IFRS-consolidated vs national-GAAP-individual split drawn throughout this chapter. On the PDF route, the
account-form\,/\,panel-aware two-sided-balance-sheet handling (\S\ref{sec:pdf-classification}) recognises
both facing halves as \emph{one} statement.

\section{Spain}
\label{sec:esef-spain}

Spanish listed groups on EU regulated markets file their consolidated IFRS annual report as ESEF iXBRL with
the \textbf{CNMV} (\emph{Comisión Nacional del Mercado de Valores}); they are also carried by
filings.xbrl.org (unlike Germany). As with the other countries a national-GAAP layer sits underneath --- the
\textbf{PGC} single-entity accounts, filed at the \emph{Registro Mercantil} and typically PDF-only (its
balance-sheet \emph{presentation} --- and a common misconception about it --- is discussed in
\S\ref{sec:esef-spain-tshape}). The three worked examples below span three sectors --- \textbf{Inditex}
(retail), \textbf{Banco Santander} (bank), \textbf{Iberdrola} (utility) --- one country, one ESEF/IFRS
regime, three statement vocabularies; all figures below were read from the filed reports.

\subsection{Two accounting flavours: PGC and IFRS}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Spanish GAAP --- the PGC} (\emph{Plan General de Contabilidad}) --- the \emph{single-entity}
    statutory accounts (\emph{cuentas anuales}): the \emph{Balance} (\emph{Activo}, \emph{Patrimonio neto y
    Pasivo}), the \emph{Cuenta de pérdidas y ganancias}, the \emph{Estado de cambios en el patrimonio neto},
    the \emph{Estado de flujos de efectivo} and the \emph{Memoria} (notes). Filed at the \emph{Registro
    Mercantil}; typically PDF. Lighter variants: \emph{PGC de PYMES} (SMEs) and a micro-enterprise regime.
    (On its balance-sheet presentation, see \S\ref{sec:esef-spain-tshape}.)
  \item \textbf{IFRS} --- the \emph{consolidated} group accounts (\emph{cuentas anuales consolidadas}):
    \emph{Estado de situación financiera} (or \emph{Balance}), \emph{Cuenta de resultados}, \emph{Estado del
    resultado global}, \emph{Estado de flujos de efectivo}, \emph{Estado (total) de cambios en el patrimonio
    neto}. This is the ESEF-tagged report; each of the three examples tags the full \textbf{five}-statement
    set (\texttt{BalanceSheet}, \texttt{IncomeStatement}, \texttt{ComprehensiveIncome}, \texttt{Equity},
    \texttt{CashFlow}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Statement vocabulary}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Spanish statement vocabulary (IFRS-consolidated and PGC terms).}
\label{tab:es-vocab}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.52\textwidth}p{0.42\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Spanish} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\emph{Estado de situación financiera} / \emph{Balance} & Balance sheet \\
\emph{Activo} / \emph{Patrimonio neto y Pasivo} & Assets side / equity-and-liabilities side \\
\emph{Cuenta de resultados} / \emph{Cuenta de pérdidas y ganancias} & Income statement \\
\emph{Estado del resultado global} & Statement of comprehensive income \\
\emph{Estado de flujos de efectivo} & Cash-flow statement \\
\emph{Estado (total) de cambios en el patrimonio neto} & Statement of changes in equity \\
\emph{Memoria} / \emph{Notas} & Notes to the accounts \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Three IFRS income-statement vocabularies, one country.} The income statement is where sector
diverges most, and the three examples cover the three \texttt{ifrs-full} presentations (read from the filed
reports):
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Inditex (retail)} --- \emph{function-of-expense}: \texttt{ifrs-full:Revenue} \(\to\)
    \texttt{CostOfSales} \(\to\) \texttt{GrossProfit} \(\to\) \texttt{OperatingExpense} (18 income-statement
    concepts, \(\approx\)500 facts total).
  \item \textbf{Iberdrola (utility)} --- \emph{nature-of-expense}: \texttt{ifrs-full:Revenue},
    \texttt{RawMaterialsAndConsumablesUsed}, \texttt{EmployeeBenefitsExpense},
    \texttt{OtherWorkPerformedByEntityAndCapitalised} (27 concepts, \(\approx\)650 facts).
  \item \textbf{Banco Santander (bank)} --- the banking layout, with \emph{no} revenue/cost-of-sales:
    \texttt{ifrs-full:RevenueFromInterest}, \texttt{InterestExpense}, \texttt{FeeAndCommissionIncome} (41
    income-statement concepts, \(\approx\)1{,}700 facts --- banks disclose far more). This is the
    \texttt{ifrs-full} financial-institution vocabulary, not a Spanish specificity, but the trio shows it
    cleanly.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Format Particularities (for extraction)}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Issuer extensions are a large, locally-named share.} Roughly a quarter to a third of tagged
    facts are issuer \emph{extension} concepts (Inditex \(\approx\)166/504, Santander \(\approx\)501/1716,
    Iberdrola \(\approx\)165/655), and they carry the \emph{filer's} language: Iberdrola's are Spanish
    (e.g.\ \texttt{ibe:GastosDeServicios}), Inditex's English (\texttt{itx:GrossProfitMargin}). Do not assume
    an English concept local-name; the \texttt{standard\_concept}/W4 bridge is what normalises them.
  \item \textbf{Filing language varies.} Inditex and Santander filed English-labelled packages (\texttt{-en}),
    Iberdrola Spanish (\texttt{-es}). The standard \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts still resolve to their taxonomy
    labels; only the extension concepts are in the filing language.
  \item \textbf{Number locale.} Comma decimal with a \textbf{dot} (or space) thousands separator ---
    \texttt{1.234.567,89} = 1{,}234{,}567.89 (like Germany, unlike France's space); amounts usually
    \emph{en miles} or \emph{en millones de euros}.
  \item \textbf{``Balance'' vs ``Estado de situación financiera''.} Like the French \emph{Bilan}, Spanish IFRS
    filers may keep \emph{Balance} rather than the literal IFRS \emph{Estado de situación financiera} --- match
    both, loosely.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Balance-sheet presentation: report-form, not the ``T''}
\label{sec:esef-spain-tshape}

A common assumption is that Spanish accounts use an \textbf{account-form} (``\emph{T}''-shaped) balance sheet
--- \emph{Activo} on one side, \emph{Patrimonio neto y Pasivo} on the other. For \emph{modern} Spanish GAAP
this is \textbf{not} the case: the PGC~2007 \emph{modelo de balance} is \textbf{vertical (report-form)} ---
\emph{ACTIVO} (\emph{A}~no corriente, \emph{B}~corriente) listed first, then \emph{PATRIMONIO NETO Y PASIVO}
(\emph{A}~patrimonio neto, \emph{B}~pasivo no corriente, \emph{C}~pasivo corriente) below it; the horizontal
``account'' form belongs to the \emph{pre-2007} plans. Checked against a real filing: in Iberdrola's
individual accounts the \emph{Activo} and \emph{Patrimonio neto y Pasivo} blocks render as \emph{separate,
stacked} tables --- vertical, not one two-sided table. The genuine account-form ``T'' is a \textbf{continental}
trait mandated by law in \textbf{Italy}, \textbf{Germany} and \textbf{France}
(\S\ref{sec:esef-italy-tshape}, \S\ref{sec:esef-germany-tshape}, \S\ref{sec:esef-france-tshape}) --- but
\emph{not} in modern Spanish GAAP.

\section{Netherlands}
\label{sec:esef-nl}

Dutch listed groups on EU regulated markets file their consolidated IFRS annual report as ESEF iXBRL with the
\textbf{AFM} (\emph{Autoriteit Financi\"ele Markten}); they are also carried by filings.xbrl.org (unlike
Germany). The national-GAAP layer sits underneath in the \emph{Dutch Civil Code} --- \textbf{Title~9 of
Book~2 BW} (\emph{Burgerlijk Wetboek}), fleshed out by the \textbf{RJ} guidelines (\emph{Richtlijnen voor de
jaarverslaggeving}, issued by the \emph{Raad voor de Jaarverslaggeving}) --- with the single-entity statutory
accounts filed at the \textbf{KvK} (\emph{Kamer van Koophandel}, the Chamber of Commerce), typically as SBR/PDF;
its balance-sheet \emph{presentation} is discussed in \S\ref{sec:esef-nl-tshape}. The three worked examples
below span three sectors --- \textbf{ASML} (semiconductors), \textbf{ING Groep} (bank), \textbf{Heineken}
(brewer) --- one country, one ESEF/IFRS regime, three statement vocabularies. Every figure below was read from
the filed report, and each matched the filings.xbrl.org (Arelle) value-parity oracle exactly: \textbf{ASML
380/380}, \textbf{ING 396/396}, \textbf{Heineken 294/294} facts.

\subsection{Two accounting flavours: Dutch GAAP and IFRS}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Dutch GAAP --- Title~9 Book~2 BW + RJ} --- the \emph{single-entity} statutory accounts
    (\emph{jaarrekening}): the \emph{balans} (\emph{activa} / \emph{passiva}, the latter being \emph{eigen
    vermogen en schulden}), the \emph{winst-en-verliesrekening}, the \emph{kasstroomoverzicht} and the
    \emph{toelichting} (notes). Filed at the KvK; typically SBR (the Dutch inline-XBRL profile) or PDF for the
    smallest filers. Size regimes under art.~2:396--398 BW (\emph{klein} / \emph{middelgroot} / \emph{groot})
    scale the disclosure, with lighter RJk guidance for small entities.
  \item \textbf{IFRS} --- the \emph{consolidated} group accounts (\emph{geconsolideerde jaarrekening}):
    \emph{geconsolideerde balans}, \emph{geconsolideerde winst-en-verliesrekening}, \emph{overzicht van het
    totaalresultaat}, \emph{geconsolideerd kasstroomoverzicht}, \emph{mutatieoverzicht van het eigen
    vermogen}. This is the ESEF-tagged report that AFM collects; all three examples tag the full IFRS
    statement set.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Statement vocabulary}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Dutch statement vocabulary (IFRS-consolidated and Title~9 terms).}
\label{tab:nl-vocab}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.50\textwidth}p{0.44\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Dutch} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\emph{Balans} & Balance sheet \\
\emph{Activa} / \emph{Passiva} & Assets side / equity-and-liabilities side \\
\emph{Eigen vermogen} / \emph{Schulden} & Equity / liabilities \\
\emph{Winst-en-verliesrekening} & Income statement (profit-and-loss account) \\
\emph{Overzicht van het totaalresultaat} & Statement of comprehensive income \\
\emph{Kasstroomoverzicht} & Cash-flow statement \\
\emph{Mutatieoverzicht van het eigen vermogen} & Statement of changes in equity \\
\emph{Toelichting} & Notes to the accounts \\
\emph{Jaarrekening} / \emph{Jaarverslag} & Financial statements / annual report \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Three IFRS income-statement vocabularies, one country.} The income statement is where sector
diverges most, and the three examples cover the three \texttt{ifrs-full} presentations (read from the filed
reports):
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{ASML (semiconductors)} --- \emph{function-of-expense}, with a product/service split:
    \texttt{ifrs-full:RevenueFromContractsWithCustomers} split into \texttt{asml:NetSystemSales} /
    \texttt{asml:NetServiceAndFieldOptionSales}, \(\to\) \texttt{ifrs-full:CostOfSales} (itemised
    \texttt{asml:CostOfSystemSales} / \texttt{asml:CostOfServiceAndFieldOptionsSales}) \(\to\)
    \texttt{GrossProfit} \(\to\) \texttt{ResearchAndDevelopmentExpense},
    \texttt{SellingGeneralAndAdministrativeExpense} \(\to\) \texttt{ProfitLossFromOperatingActivities} (24
    issuer extensions, 606 facts).
  \item \textbf{Heineken (brewer)} --- \emph{nature-of-expense}, with a beverage-industry line:
    \texttt{ifrs-full:Revenue} and the extension \texttt{heia:RevenueLessExciseTaxExpense} (revenue net of
    excise duties), then \texttt{EmployeeBenefitsExpense} and depreciation/amortisation as expense categories;
    excise itself sits in \texttt{ifrs-full:TaxExpenseOtherThanIncomeTaxExpense} (25 extensions, 371 facts).
  \item \textbf{ING Groep (bank)} --- the banking layout, with \emph{no} revenue/cost-of-sales:
    \texttt{ifrs-full:InterestRevenueCalculatedUsingEffectiveInterestMethod}, \texttt{InterestExpense} and net
    \texttt{InterestRevenueExpense}; \texttt{FeeAndCommissionIncome} / \texttt{FeeAndCommissionExpense};
    \texttt{ing:ValuationResultsAndNetTradingIncome}; and loan-loss via
    \texttt{ing:AdditionToLoanLossProvision} (33 extensions, 553 facts --- the balance sheet is dominated by
    \texttt{FinancialAssetsAtFairValueThrough\ldots} measurement categories). This is the \texttt{ifrs-full}
    financial-institution vocabulary, not a Dutch specificity, but the trio shows it cleanly.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Format Particularities (for extraction)}

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{The extension prefix is the issuer's ticker/short-code.} Each filer's extension namespace is
    its own short code --- \texttt{asml:}, \texttt{ing:}, \texttt{heia:} (Heineken's Euronext ticker HEIA) ---
    and the extension concepts are \textbf{English}-named (\texttt{heia:RevenueLessExciseTaxExpense},
    \texttt{ing:AdditionToLoanLossProvision}), unlike the Spanish filers whose extensions may be in the local
    language. Roughly 24--33 extension concepts per filer; the \texttt{standard\_concept}/W4 bridge normalises
    them.
  \item \textbf{Industry-specific extensions carry the sector.} The single most telling concept in each filing
    is an extension: Heineken's \texttt{heia:RevenueLessExciseTaxExpense} (a beverage \emph{net-revenue} line),
    ING's fair-value-through-P\&L financial-instrument categories. Do not expect these in \texttt{ifrs-full}.
  \item \textbf{Filing language is English.} These Dutch multinationals file English-labelled packages; the
    \texttt{narratives()} text-blocks come through as \texttt{ifrs-full:Disclosure\ldots Explanatory} (ASML 55,
    ING 87, Heineken 74), the IFRS \emph{Explanatory} convention (\S\ref{sec:api-narratives}), not the
    \texttt{TextBlock} suffix.
  \item \textbf{Number locale.} In the human-readable iXBRL the Dutch convention is comma decimal with a
    \textbf{dot} thousands separator --- \texttt{1.234.567,89} = 1{,}234{,}567.89 (like Germany and Spain) ---
    though the tagged XBRL values are locale-normalised, so extraction is unaffected.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Balance-sheet presentation: a statutory \emph{choice} of formats}
\label{sec:esef-nl-tshape}

Where Spain mandates the vertical form (\S\ref{sec:esef-spain-tshape}) and Italy, Germany and France the
account form (\S\ref{sec:esef-italy-tshape}, \S\ref{sec:esef-germany-tshape}, \S\ref{sec:esef-france-tshape}),
Dutch law leaves it to the filer. The \emph{Besluit modellen jaarrekening}
(BMJ, the Model Financial Statements Decree under Title~9) prescribes the layouts and offers two balance-sheet
models: \textbf{Model~A} (\emph{vertical / report-form}, \emph{activa} then \emph{passiva} stacked) and
\textbf{Model~B} (\emph{horizontal / account-form}, the two-sided ``\emph{T}''); the profit-and-loss account
is Model~E or F, with lighter models (C/D, I/J) for small companies under art.~2:396~BW.\footnote{\emph{Besluit
modellen jaarrekening} (BWBR0003648), bijlagen; see also \emph{Title~9 Book~2 BW}, art.~2:361\,ff. The BMJ has
the status of law and applies to \emph{N.V.}\ and \emph{B.V.}\ entities (partially to banks and insurers).}
So the account-form ``\emph{T}'' is \emph{permitted} in the Netherlands but not compelled --- in practice large
listed groups present the vertical Model~A, and the ESEF/IFRS consolidated statements the three examples file
are the IAS~1 vertical layout throughout.

\section{Sweden}


\section{Workflow Use Case Examples}
\label{sec:esef-example}

This section collects practical ESEF workflows --- a few lines each --- spanning discovery, extraction,
prose search and validation. The worked example below runs end-to-end on a real filer; more will follow.

\noindent The ESEF pieces compose into a full workflow --- the analogue of the SEC filings life-cycle
(\S\ref{sec:lifecycle-example}), but spanning discovery, extraction, \emph{prose search}, and
\emph{validation}. The script below runs end-to-end on a real filer --- Signify N.V.\ (Dutch, IFRS) ---
using only the public API, then (a) searches the report's text and (b) cross-checks the extracted
numbers against two independent references --- a parser oracle and an external benchmark
(\S\ref{sec:validation}).

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> using EDGAR
julia> set_user_agent("Your Name you@example.com")

# 1. Discover the issuer's ESEF report packages by LEI (via filings.xbrl.org).
julia> hs = discover(FilingsXBRLOrg(); lei = "549300072P3J1X8NZO35")  # Signify N.V.
julia> h  = first(filter(x -> endswith(x.url, "-en.zip"), hs))         # the English package

# 2. Fetch the report package (downloads + reads the ZIP into a Filing).
julia> f = fetch_filing(h)              # Filing(:ixbrl, ...); f.entity == EntityId(:lei, ...)

# 3. Extract and classify the IFRS facts; pull a face statement.
julia> rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true)
julia> select_statement(rows, "BalanceSheet")     # ifrs-full:Assets, Liabilities, Equity, ...

# 4. (a) Search the prose: locate paragraphs by phrase, then jump back via the index.
julia> hits = find_paragraphs(f, "sustainability")
julia> EDGAR._paragraphs(f.content)[hits[1].index]    # the located paragraph

# 5. (b) Validate the numbers against independent references (see "Validating the Extraction").
#     Parser oracle -- Arelle xBRL-JSON, per statement:
julia> include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "arelle_esef_oracle.jl"))
julia> using .ArelleEsefOracle
julia> ArelleEsefOracle.statements("549300072P3J1X8NZO35")    # BS / IS / CF fact parity

#     External benchmark -- Yahoo Finance totals (needs YFinance in the environment):
julia> using YFinance
julia> include(joinpath(pkgdir(EDGAR), "src", "sources", "yahoo_oracle.jl"))
julia> using .YahooOracle
julia> YahooOracle.validate(:esef, "549300072P3J1X8NZO35", "LIGHT.AS")
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent Every step succeeds against the live filing; Table~\ref{tab:esef-lifecycle} records what each
produces. Steps 1--3 are the extraction pipeline, step 4 is text search, and step 5 is the
``trust-but-verify'' cross-check --- both references agree with the extraction across all three statements.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{The ESEF workflow, step by step (Signify N.V.).}
\label{tab:esef-lifecycle}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Call} & \textbf{Result} \\
\midrule
1. Discover by LEI         & \texttt{discover(FilingsXBRLOrg(); lei)}        & report-package handles (per year / language) \\
2. Fetch package           & \texttt{fetch\_filing(h)}                       & \texttt{Filing(:ixbrl)}, LEI identity \\
3. Facts + classify        & \texttt{facts(f; classify=true)} $\to$ \texttt{select\_statement} & IFRS facts across BS / IS / CF \\
4. Search prose            & \texttt{find\_paragraphs(f, "sustainability")} & matching paragraphs, each with its \texttt{index} \\
5a. Validate (Arelle)      & \texttt{ArelleEsefOracle.statements(lei)}           & BS 76/76, IS 42/42, CF 70/70 (100\%) \\
5b. Validate (Yahoo)       & \texttt{YahooOracle.validate(:esef, lei, "LIGHT.AS")} & Assets / Equity / Revenue / Net income / Oper.\ cash flow all match \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}


\chapter{United Kingdom Filing Systems}
\label{sys:uk}

The United Kingdom exposes company financial reports through \emph{two distinct channels}, and
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} reaches both. They are not alternatives bolted together --- they are different
\texttt{FilingSource}s (\S\ref{sec:architecture}) that serve different filer populations and, often,
the same company in different formats. This chapter covers both and gives a worked workflow for each.

\section{Entities covered}
\label{sec:uk-channels}

The UK exposes company reports through two channels, each serving a different filer population
(Table~\ref{tab:uk-entities}): the registrar (every UK company) and the FCA's store of regulated-market
disclosures (listed issuers only).

\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\textbf{(a)}] \textbf{Companies House (CH)} --- the UK \emph{registrar}. \emph{Every} UK
    company must file annual accounts here, so CH spans the whole population (public \emph{and}
    private). A company is identified by its \textbf{registration number}
    (\texttt{EntityId(:companies\_house,\ \ldots)}), and accounts are tagged against UK GAAP
    (FRS~101/102/105, the FRC taxonomy) or IFRS. A CH filing carries \textbf{no issuer extension
    taxonomy}, so labels and classification \emph{delegate} to the published \emph{standard} taxonomy
    for a concept's prefix. Access is the Companies House API (free key), with a keyless
    whole-registrar bulk product as an alternative.

  \item[\textbf{(b)}] \textbf{The National Storage Mechanism (NSM)} --- the FCA's official store of
    \emph{regulated-market} disclosures. It covers only \textbf{listed issuers}, whose
    \textbf{Annual Financial Reports} are filed as \textbf{UKSEF} --- the UK profile of ESEF
    (\S\ref{sys:esef}): inline-XBRL report packages, \texttt{ifrs-full} plus an issuer extension,
    identified by \textbf{LEI}. Because UKSEF \emph{is} ESEF in form, these parse through the shared
    ESEF report-package path unchanged; the NSM supplies only its own discovery and fetch. Access is a
    keyless JSON API.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{The UK filer types, by channel, and what \texttt{EDGAR.jl} does with each.}
\label{tab:uk-entities}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.24\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}p{0.28\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Entity type} & \textbf{Identity} & \textbf{Channel} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
All UK-registered companies (public \& private) & registration number & Companies House & accounts: iXBRL parsed, PDF a typed outcome \\
Regulated-market issuers (listed) & LEI & NSM (UKSEF) & UKSEF report parsed via the shared ESEF path \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\section{Forms and statement types}
\label{sec:uk-forms}

\paragraph{Format by channel and filer.} The same UKSEF annual report is reachable from \emph{two}
sources --- the NSM directly, and the \texttt{FilingsXBRLOrg} aggregator (\S\ref{sys:esef}), which
indexes the NSM's UK data. Going to the NSM directly removes the dependency on the aggregator.
Table~\ref{tab:uk-channels} summarises what each channel yields.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{UK financial reports by channel and filer type. The NSM and FilingsXBRLOrg columns are the
\emph{same} UKSEF document fetched from different sources.}
\label{tab:uk-channels}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.24\textwidth}p{0.22\textwidth}p{0.22\textwidth}p{0.20\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{UKSEF $\cdot$ NSM} (FCA direct) & \textbf{UKSEF $\cdot$ FilingsXBRLOrg} (aggregator) & \textbf{Companies House} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Public (listed)} \newline financials / text & iXBRL \emph{and} PDF & iXBRL: yes / yes & PDF: yes / yes \\
\textbf{Private} \newline financials / text & --- (not on NSM) & --- (not on NSM) & iXBRL \emph{or} PDF \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The NSM holds both formats too.} The NSM is not an iXBRL-only store: its Annual Financial
Reports are \emph{mostly} PDF, with a machine-readable UKSEF (inline-XBRL) copy added only for recent
financial years (FY2021 onwards, when ESEF tagging became mandatory). The \emph{same} annual report
can therefore appear on the NSM as \emph{two} records --- one PDF and one inline-XBRL package --- under
the same period end. Only the tagged UKSEF packages are reachable through the adapter today
(\S\ref{sys:nsm}); the PDF copies belong to the same PDF/text track as Companies House PDFs.

\paragraph{The format is not predictable in advance.} For Companies House, a given company's accounts
may be inline XBRL \emph{or} PDF (the document metadata can even offer both for one filing), and
\emph{which} it is cannot be inferred from the company's size or status --- the only firm
regularity observed is that \emph{listed} issuers file PDF at CH (their machine-readable copy lives on
the NSM as UKSEF). So the rule is simple: \textbf{fetch the filing and read \texttt{f.kind}}
(Table~\ref{tab:ch-formats}); a PDF is a typed, non-fatal outcome, not an error.
The relative prevalence of PDF-only, iXBRL-only and dual-format accounts across the registrar has not
been measured here and would require a representative sample; it is left as an open question.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{The three Companies House document outcomes. \texttt{fetch\_filing} content-negotiates in
preference order iXBRL \(\rightarrow\) XBRL \(\rightarrow\) PDF (a single filing's metadata may offer
more than one), and reports which it got as \texttt{f.kind}. Unlike the NSM, Companies House serves only
what the filer submitted --- it does not generate xBRL-CSV/xBRL-JSON renderings.}
\label{tab:ch-formats}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.12\textwidth}p{0.26\textwidth}p{0.28\textwidth}p{0.20\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\texttt{f.kind}} & \textbf{Document API serves as} & \textbf{What it is} & \textbf{EDGAR.jl} \\
\midrule
\texttt{:ixbrl} & \texttt{application/xhtml+xml} & Inline-XBRL accounts (FRS/IFRS) & parsed $\to$ \texttt{facts} \\
\texttt{:xbrl}  & \texttt{application/xml}       & Classic XBRL instance            & parsed $\to$ \texttt{facts} \\
\texttt{:pdf}   & \texttt{application/pdf} (or no structured resource) & Paper/PDF accounts & typed outcome, not parsed (PDF track) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Statement types}
UK accounts are tagged under one of two taxonomy families: \textbf{UK GAAP} --- the FRC's FRS~101/102/105
taxonomy --- or \textbf{IFRS} (\texttt{ifrs-full}). Disclosure scope varies with the standard: a
micro-entity (FRS~105) filing is the most abridged, while FRS~102 and IFRS accounts carry the full balance
sheet and income statement, with cash-flow and changes-in-equity statements where required. A Companies
House filing carries \emph{no issuer extension taxonomy}, so labels and classification \emph{delegate} to
the published \emph{standard} taxonomy for each concept's prefix; a UKSEF report on the NSM instead tags
against \texttt{ifrs-full} plus an issuer extension, exactly as ESEF. When a filing is served as PDF rather
than (i)XBRL (Table~\ref{tab:ch-formats}), no tagged statement set is produced and it belongs to the
PDF/text track.

\section{Finding a company}
\label{sec:uk-company-search}

Both UK workflows start from an identifier --- a registration number for Companies House, an LEI for
the NSM --- that you rarely know by heart. \texttt{company\_search} is the lookup step \emph{before}
discovery: it turns a name (or a set of criteria) into those identifiers. Like \texttt{discover}, it is
a generic verb dispatched on the source, and every source returns the \emph{same} row type, a
\texttt{CompanyHit}:

\begin{lstlisting}
const CompanyHit = @NamedTuple{name::String, id::EntityId, status::String, type::String,
                              incorporated::Union{Date,Nothing}, sic::Vector{String},
                              address::String, country::String}
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The vector of hits is a Tables.jl row table (drops straight into \texttt{DataFrames}, CSV,
DuckDB), and \texttt{hit.id} is the system-tagged identity --- pass \texttt{hit.id.value} into
\texttt{discover}. Fields a given registry does not publish are left empty.

\paragraph{Companies House --- by name or by criteria.}
\begin{lstlisting}
company_search(CompaniesHouseApi(), "tesco")                 # ranked free-text (name or number)
# Any criterion switches to the advanced search (filters server-side):
company_search(CompaniesHouseApi(), "tesco";
               status = "active", type = "plc", sic = "47110",
               incorporated_from = Date(1900), incorporated_to = Date(1990))
only(company_search(CompaniesHouseApi(), "445790")).id.value # -> "00445790"
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent With only a query it is the registry's ranked search over name and number; supplying any of
\texttt{status}, \texttt{type}, \texttt{sic}, \texttt{incorporated\_from}/\texttt{incorporated\_to},
\texttt{dissolved\_from}/\texttt{dissolved\_to} or \texttt{location} switches to the advanced endpoint,
which filters server-side. Hits populate the full \texttt{CompanyHit} (status, type, incorporation date,
SIC, address).

\paragraph{NSM --- name to LEI.} The NSM is a disclosure store, not a registry, so its search resolves
a name to the issuer's LEI:
\begin{lstlisting}
hit = only(company_search(NSM(), "tesco"))      # id.value = the LEI; registrar fields empty
discover(NSM(); lei = hit.id.value, year = 2025)
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent By default it scopes to the inline-XBRL Annual Financial Reports (the filings that carry an
LEI \emph{and} that the adapter can fetch), deduplicated to one row per issuer; the registrar facets
(\texttt{status}, \texttt{type}, \texttt{incorporated}, \texttt{sic}, \texttt{address}) are therefore
empty. A consequence of that scoping: an issuer that has filed \emph{only} PDF annual reports --- which
includes everything before FY2021 --- has no tagged record to match, so it does not resolve here.

\section{Workflow Use Case Examples}
\label{sec:uk-workflows}

This section gives a worked workflow for each UK channel --- a few lines each --- from identity through
fetch to extraction; more examples will follow.

\subsection{Companies House workflow}
\label{sys:companieshouse}

Identity is the registration number. Set the API key once (or the
\texttt{COMPANIES\_HOUSE\_API\_KEY} environment variable), discover a company's accounts, fetch, and
--- when the filing is XBRL --- run the same system-agnostic pipeline as everywhere else.

\begin{lstlisting}
using EDGAR
set_credentials(CompaniesHouse(); api_key = ENV["COMPANIES_HOUSE_API_KEY"])

# Discover a company's accounts by registration number (newest first).
hs = discover(CompaniesHouseApi(); company_number = "00021497")

# Narrow within a company: a filing-type code and a period-end date range or single year.
hs = discover(CompaniesHouseApi(); company_number = "00445790", type = "AA", from = Date(2020))
hs = discover(CompaniesHouseApi(); company_number = "00445790", type = "AA", year = 2023)

f  = fetch_filing(first(hs))      # a Filing; always inspect f.kind before parsing
f.kind                            # :ixbrl | :xbrl | :pdf  -- not predictable in advance

if f.kind in (:ixbrl, :xbrl)
    rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true)   # FRS/IFRS facts (uk-core, ...)
    select_statement(rows, "BalanceSheet")
end

# Keyless alternative: the whole-registrar bulk Accounts Data Product (no API key).
hs = discover(CompaniesHouseBulk(); date = "2026-06-19", company_number = "00021497")
f  = fetch_filing(first(hs))
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent \texttt{discover(CompaniesHouseApi())} filters server-side by \texttt{category} (default
\texttt{"accounts"}) and, within a company, narrows further by \texttt{type} (a filing-type code such
as \texttt{"AA"} for full accounts) and the accounting period end --- either a \texttt{from}/\texttt{to}
range or a single \texttt{year} (the same date filter the NSM offers, \S\ref{sys:nsm}).
Labels and classification delegate to the published FRC \emph{standard} taxonomy (the
filing bundles no extension), so \texttt{labels=true} resolves FRS concept labels with no per-filing
linkbase. A filing whose accounts are PDF returns \texttt{f.kind == :pdf} with the content URL
retained; structured facts are not extracted from it (PDF extraction is a separate track).

\subsection{NSM (UKSEF) workflow}
\label{sys:nsm}

Identity is the LEI. The NSM serves listed issuers' \textbf{Annual Financial Reports} as UKSEF
report packages, which parse through the shared ESEF path. Browse the category vocabulary, discover by
LEI, then fetch and extract:

\begin{lstlisting}
using EDGAR
set_user_agent("Your Name you@example.com")   # for the UKSEF report-package download

nsm_categories("annual")                       # ["ACS" => "Annual Financial Report", ...]
hs = discover(NSM(); lei = "2138002P5RNKC5W2JZ46", year = 2025)   # Tesco PLC
f  = fetch_filing(first(hs))                   # Filing(:ixbrl) -- a UKSEF report package
rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true)
select_statement(rows, "IncomeStatement")      # ifrs-full / issuer-extension revenue, ...
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent \texttt{discover(NSM())} accepts a friendly \texttt{category} (resolved against the active
NSM category vocabulary by \texttt{nsm\_categories}) or a category code directly, and narrows the
accounting-reference date by either \texttt{year} (a single calendar year) or an explicit
\texttt{from}/\texttt{to} window of \texttt{Date}s --- the same within-filer date filter Companies
House offers (\S\ref{sys:companieshouse}). Only the tagged UKSEF packages are XBRL-tagged on the NSM;
the other formats (Table~\ref{tab:nsm-formats}) are untagged xHTML or PDF and raise an informative
error today --- they belong to the same PDF/text track noted for Companies House. The very same UKSEF
package is also reachable via \texttt{discover(FilingsXBRLOrg(); lei)} (\S\ref{sys:esef}); the two are
independent sources for one document, and the Arelle oracle (\S\ref{sec:validation}) cross-checks them.

\paragraph{Three document formats.} The NSM tags every document with a \texttt{tag\_esef} state, and
each maps to a distinct format (Table~\ref{tab:nsm-formats}). The format is \emph{not} fixed by the
kind of filing: an Annual Financial Report can appear in all three states --- even for the same issuer
and period (we observed both a tagged package and a PDF for the same Tesco financial year).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{The three document formats the NSM serves, by \texttt{tag\_esef} state. Counts are
\emph{documents} (one disclosure each), not distinct filers --- an issuer accumulates one tagged AFR
per year since the FY2021 tagging mandate, so the \(\sim\)2{,}900 tagged documents span considerably
fewer companies.}
\label{tab:nsm-formats}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.12\textwidth}p{0.21\textwidth}p{0.15\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\texttt{tag\_esef}} & \textbf{Format} & \textbf{Extension} & \textbf{What it is} \\
\midrule
\texttt{"Tagged"}   & \textbf{iXBRL} (ESEF report package) & \texttt{.zip}                  & Machine-readable Annual Financial Reports (\(\sim\)2{,}900 docs). \\
\texttt{"Untagged"} & \textbf{xHTML} (no XBRL tags)         & \texttt{.xhtml}/\texttt{.html} & Annual Financial Reports as structured HTML, untagged (\(\sim\)1{,}700 docs). \\
\texttt{""} (empty) & \textbf{PDF} and \textbf{RNS HTML}    & \texttt{.pdf}/\texttt{.html}   & Annual reports filed as PDF, plus the entire RNS announcement stream (millions of docs). \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent The empty state is dominated by \textbf{RNS} --- the London Stock Exchange's
\emph{Regulatory News Service}, the channel through which listed issuers disclose regulatory news
(director dealings, shareholding notifications, dividends, trading updates, \ldots). A company issues
dozens of RNS items a year but only one annual report, so RNS HTML vastly outnumbers everything else;
it carries no financial statements and is out of scope for extraction. Of the three formats, only the
\textbf{tagged} iXBRL packages are machine-readable financials --- which is why the adapter fetches
those and leaves the xHTML and PDF/RNS tracks to a future text/PDF extractor.

\paragraph{Download formats on the FCA website.} Table~\ref{tab:nsm-formats} is what an issuer
\emph{files}; the FCA website (\texttt{data.fca.org.uk}) offers a richer download menu for the
machine-readable ones (Table~\ref{tab:nsm-downloads}). For a \textbf{Tagged} Annual Financial Report you
can download the original inline-XBRL package \emph{or} the same tagged facts re-serialised as
\textbf{xBRL-CSV} or \textbf{xBRL-JSON} --- alternative encodings of one report, generated by the FCA
from the inline-XBRL (the xBRL-JSON is the very serialisation the Arelle oracle consumes,
\S\ref{sec:validation}). \emph{Untagged} and \emph{PDF} documents offer only their original file. So an
AFR is filed as exactly one of \{iXBRL, xHTML, PDF\}, and only the tagged (iXBRL) one additionally
yields xBRL-CSV/xBRL-JSON; the other categories are HTML or PDF and never carry XBRL.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{What the FCA website offers for download, by document. The xBRL-CSV/xBRL-JSON renderings exist
\emph{only} for Tagged AFRs (they are generated from the inline-XBRL); they are a convenience of the FCA
site, not something \texttt{EDGAR.jl} produces --- the adapter fetches the inline-XBRL package and parses
it with the same jurisdiction-agnostic extractor used everywhere else.}
\label{tab:nsm-downloads}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.34\textwidth}p{0.24\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Document} & \textbf{Filed as} & \textbf{Also downloadable as} \\
\midrule
Annual Financial Report --- \textbf{Tagged}   & iXBRL (XHTML+XBRL) & xBRL-CSV, xBRL-JSON \\
Annual Financial Report --- \textbf{Untagged} & xHTML              & --- \\
Annual Financial Report --- \textbf{PDF}      & PDF                & --- \\
Any other category (RNS, quarterly, half-year, \ldots) & HTML or PDF & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Coverage caveat.} Because discovery filters to the tagged UKSEF packages, an issuer or
financial year with \emph{only} a PDF report --- all years before FY2021, and many issuers since ---
returns an \emph{empty} result, not an error. The \texttt{year} filter is also blind to undated PDFs:
on the NSM the accounting period end is recorded only for the tagged packages, so PDF records carry no
period date to filter on (they retain submission and publication dates instead). Both limitations lift
once the PDF/text track lands.

\begin{subappendices}
\section{Credentials}\label{sec:uk-credentials}

\textbf{Companies House} requires a \emph{free} API key (register on the CH developer portal). Set it per
session, or read it from the environment:

\begin{lstlisting}
set_credentials(CompaniesHouse(); api_key = ENV["COMPANIES_HOUSE_API_KEY"])
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The \textbf{NSM} (UKSEF) JSON API is \textbf{keyless}; only the report-package download needs a
SEC-style \texttt{User-Agent} (\S\ref{sec:ua}), set via \texttt{set\_user\_agent}.
\end{subappendices}

\chapter{Japan: EDINET}
\label{sys:edinet}

EDINET is Japan's FSA disclosure system, and the fourth \texttt{FilingSystem}. A filing is a
\textbf{submission ZIP} of inline XBRL plus its bundled taxonomy --- the same shape as a UKSEF/ESEF
report package --- so it parses through the \emph{shared} report-package reader and the
jurisdiction-agnostic XBRL extractor (\S\ref{sys:esef}, \S\ref{sys:extraction}) essentially unchanged;
EDINET supplies only its API source, the ZIP entry layout (the primary instance lives under
\texttt{XBRL/PublicDoc/}), and the new vocabularies below. Identity is the EDINET code
(\texttt{EntityId(:edinet,\ "E\#\#\#\#\#")}).

\emph{Status:} the system, the offline parse, and the classification vocabularies are
\textbf{implemented and verified}; the live API discovery is implemented but awaits a key; the
language-aware labels and CSV oracle are planned (see below).

\paragraph{Formats.} EDINET serves each filing in three forms, flagged per document by the API's
\texttt{pdfFlag} / \texttt{xbrlFlag} / \texttt{csvFlag}: a human-readable \textbf{PDF} (\texttt{type=2},
out of scope for \texttt{facts()}); the \textbf{inline-XBRL submission ZIP} (\texttt{type=1}) --- the bytes
\texttt{facts(f)} parses; and a \textbf{CSV} of the tagged facts (\texttt{type=5}), the regulator's own
parse used as our oracle (\S\ref{sec:edinet-oracle}). EDINET's XBRL is \emph{inline} (iXBRL embedded in
\texttt{.htm} files under \texttt{XBRL/PublicDoc/}) --- in contrast to DART, whose XBRL is \emph{classic}
and which offers no CSV (\S\ref{sys:dart}).

\section{Entities covered}\label{sec:edinet-entities}

EDINET receives disclosures from three kinds of filer (Table~\ref{tab:edinet-entities}). The securities
issuers --- ordinary corporations --- file the periodic reports that carry the financial statements
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts; funds and the parties to large-shareholding and tender-offer disclosures file
their own document types. A filer is identified by its \textbf{EDINET code}
(\texttt{EntityId(:edinet,\ "E\#\#\#\#\#")}).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{EDINET filer types and what \texttt{EDGAR.jl} does with each.}
\label{tab:edinet-entities}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.26\textwidth}p{0.15\textwidth}p{0.23\textwidth}p{0.22\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Entity type} & \textbf{Example} & \textbf{Primary documents} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
Securities issuer (corporation) & Toyota (E02144) & Annual / Quarterly / Semiannual Securities Report & financial statements extracted \\
Investment fund & --- & Fund securities reports & \emph{not implemented yet} \\
Large-holding / tender-offer party & --- & Large-Volume Holding, Tender Offer documents & \emph{not implemented yet} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Securities issuers}
Ordinary listed or registered corporations. They file the periodic securities reports (annual, quarterly,
semiannual) that carry the financial statements, plus registration, governance and extraordinary
disclosures. Identity is the EDINET code (e.g.\ Toyota Motor E02144, Nintendo E01772). Their statements are
extracted in full.

\subsection{Investment funds}
Collective investment vehicles, which file fund-specific securities reports. Discovery is reachable through
the API; fact extraction for fund reports is \emph{not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Large-holding and tender-offer parties}
Investors crossing the 5\,\% large-volume-holding threshold, and the parties to a tender offer (the offeror
and the subject company). They file the large-shareholding and tender-offer documents below rather than
financial statements. Discovery only --- extraction is \emph{not implemented yet}.

\section{Forms and statement types}\label{sec:edinet-forms}

EDINET's document types group into six families (Table~\ref{tab:edinet-forms}); \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts
the periodic securities reports --- the carriers of the financial statements --- and reaches the rest for
discovery as the API matures. Each family is detailed below, followed by the statement types those reports
carry.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{EDINET document families and their support in \texttt{EDGAR.jl}.}
\label{tab:edinet-forms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.43\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Family ($\to$ subsection)} & \textbf{Documents} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
Periodic securities reports & Annual, Quarterly, Semiannual Securities Report (+ amendments) & statements extracted \\
Registration statements & Securities Registration Statement; Shelf Registration Statement / Supplements & \emph{not yet} \\
Governance \& control & Internal Control Report; Confirmation Letter; Status Report of Parent Company & \emph{not yet} \\
Large shareholding & Report of Possession of Large Volume & \emph{not yet} \\
Tender offers & Tender Offer Notification; Subject Company's Position Statement; Tender Offeror's Answer; Tender Offer Report; Written Withdrawal & \emph{not yet} \\
Other corporate actions & Share Buyback Report; Extraordinary Report & \emph{not yet} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Downloadable formats: PDF, iXBRL, classic XBRL and CSV}\label{sec:edinet-formats}
For a periodic securities report EDINET serves the \emph{same} filing in \textbf{four} forms, selected by the
document API's \texttt{type} parameter --- unusually rich (ESEF, by contrast, ships inline XBRL only):
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{(a) PDF} (\texttt{type=2}) --- the rendered report; the form \texttt{EDGAR.jl}'s PDF
    extractor consumes. Japanese EDINET PDFs carry a garbled/CID text layer, so they need forced full-page
    OCR (\S\ref{sec:pdf-pipeline}), which is the slow path.
  \item \textbf{(b) Inline XBRL (iXBRL)} --- inside the submission ZIP (\texttt{type=1}): the report as
    xHTML with embedded \texttt{ix:} tags (one \texttt{\_ixbrl.htm} per section) plus the
    label/presentation/calculation/definition linkbases and the schema.
  \item \textbf{(c) Classic XBRL} --- \emph{also} inside that ZIP: a separate \texttt{.xbrl} instance
    alongside the inline documents. So recent filings provide \emph{both} XBRL forms, not one.
  \item \textbf{(d) CSV} (\texttt{type=5}) --- the FSA's own parse of the tagged facts, the validation
    oracle of \S\ref{sec:edinet-oracle}.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Four renderings of one submission let the PDF extractor be checked against three independent,
byte-identical references. One caveat on the CSV: it is \emph{not} tables-only. It lists \emph{every} tagged
fact --- including narrative sections tagged as \textbf{text-block} facts (company history, business
description, \ldots), each a single multi-thousand-character cell of raw text/HTML. So the numeric statements
arrive as tidy per-fact rows, but prose is present only as coarse whole-section blocks, not structured
paragraphs. The CSV columns are the element ID (concept), item name (the Japanese/English label), context,
relative year, the \emph{consolidated/individual} scope marker, period-vs-instant, unit and value.

\subsection{Periodic securities reports}
The annual securities report (\emph{y\=uka sh\=oken h\=okokusho}, doc-type code \texttt{120}), the quarterly
securities report and the semiannual securities report --- each with an amendment variant. These are the
EDINET analogue of the 10-K/10-Q and the carriers of the tagged financial statements; they are extracted in
full.

\subsection{Registration statements}
The securities registration statement and the shelf registration statement with its supplements (and their
amendments), filed when offering securities. Discovery only; \emph{not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Governance and control}
The internal control report, the confirmation letter (management's attestation of the report), and the
status report of a parent company. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Large shareholding}
The report of possession of large volume (\emph{tairy\=o hoy\=u h\=okokusho}), filed on crossing the 5\,\%
beneficial-ownership threshold, with its change amendments. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Tender offers}
The tender offer notification, the subject company's position statement, the tender offeror's answer, the
tender offer report and the written withdrawal of a tender offer. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Other corporate actions}
The share buyback report and the extraordinary report (\emph{rinji h\=okokusho}, filed on material events).
\emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Statement types}

A Japanese filer's accounting basis may be \textbf{IFRS}, \textbf{Japanese GAAP} or (historically)
\textbf{US-GAAP} --- but its EDINET securities report tags the figures in just \emph{two} EDINET concept
namespaces, which is what classification keys on: \texttt{jpigp\_cor} for IFRS, and \texttt{jppfs\_cor} for
everything else (including US-GAAP-basis filers). \texttt{vocab\_jpgaap.jl} supplies both. The SEC
\texttt{us-gaap} namespace does \emph{not} appear in EDINET --- a flavour is read empirically from the
concept prefixes, and on real annual reports we see \texttt{jpigp\_cor} for Toyota Motor (7203, IFRS),
\texttt{jppfs\_cor} for Nintendo (7974, Japanese GAAP) and a megabank like Mitsubishi UFJ (8306), and
--- notably --- \texttt{jppfs\_cor} again for Canon (7751), a historic US-GAAP name whose EDINET report
is nonetheless Japanese-GAAP-tagged (its US-GAAP statements go to the SEC on a 20-F, not to EDINET):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Japanese GAAP} --- the \texttt{jppfs\_cor} namespace (Japan Public Financial Statements),
    e.g.\ \texttt{jppfs\_cor:Assets}, \texttt{jppfs\_cor:NetSales}, \texttt{jppfs\_cor:OperatingIncome},
    \texttt{jppfs\_cor:NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivities}. Unlike Companies House (which binds the
    FRC namespace to \emph{arbitrary} prefixes), EDINET filers use the \textbf{standard \texttt{jppfs\_cor}
    prefix consistently}, so no canonicalisation is needed.
  \item \textbf{IFRS} --- EDINET's own IFRS taxonomy, the \texttt{jpigp\_cor} namespace with
    \texttt{…IFRS}-suffixed local names: \texttt{jpigp\_cor:RevenueIFRS},
    \texttt{jpigp\_cor:AssetsIFRS}, \texttt{jpigp\_cor:OperatingProfitLossIFRS},
    \texttt{jpigp\_cor:ProfitLossBeforeTaxIFRS},
    \texttt{jpigp\_cor:NetCashProvidedByUsedInOperatingActivitiesIFRS}. Crucially, EDINET IFRS filers do
    \emph{not} tag with the bare \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts, so they need their own anchors --- a gap that
    only surfaced when an IFRS filer was tested, and was then filled from the verified concepts.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Both vocabularies are merged into the scored classification registry alongside \texttt{us-gaap},
\texttt{ifrs-full} and the FRC set, and the right one is selected per filing by concept prefix --- exactly
as for the other systems (\S\ref{sys:extraction}). They were \textbf{verified against four real filings}:
J-GAAP --- Kakaku.com, Yahoo Japan and TIS; IFRS --- the official FSA IFRS sample instance. The right one is
selected per filing by concept prefix, after which a filing flows through the same pipeline as every other
system (\S\ref{sec:edinet-workflows}).

\section{Discovery and labels (pending an API key)}

EDINET discovery is \textbf{date-based}: \texttt{discover(EDINETApi();\ date,\ edinet\_code,\ doc\_type)}
lists a day's submissions from the EDINET REST API and returns handles, filtering server-listed results by
EDINET code and document type (e.g.\ \texttt{"120"} = the annual securities report). It is
implemented but \textbf{awaits live verification with a free Subscription-Key} (set via
\texttt{set\_credentials(EDINET();\ api\_key)} or the \texttt{EDINET\_API\_KEY} environment variable). The
remaining pieces also wait on that key or are planned: the full \textbf{form/document-type} catalogue, the
\textbf{language-aware labels} (\texttt{label\_map(f;\ lang)} --- EDINET's label linkbase carries Japanese
\emph{and} English via \texttt{xml:lang}, the forcing case for multilingual labels), and the EDINET
\textbf{CSV fact export} as an independent parser oracle, described next.

\section{The bundled concept table: labels and concept metadata}\label{sec:edinet-concepts}

An EDINET submission tags each figure with a \emph{concept} (e.g.\ \texttt{jppfs\_cor:NetSales}) but ships
no portable \textbf{label} for it and no concept \textbf{metadata} (its item \textbf{type}, its
\textbf{balance}); all of that lives in the \emph{standard} EDINET taxonomy, a 72\,MB, 2\,700-file package
the filing only \emph{references} by URI. Re-parsing that package per filing would be absurd, so
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} pre-distils it once into a small JSON lookup table and ships that in the repository ---
the EDINET analogue of the SEC concept-map under \texttt{src/core/taxonomy/data/}.

\paragraph{What it is, and why it matters.} Each concept maps to:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \texttt{label\_en} / \texttt{label\_ja} --- the bilingual \textbf{name}, taken from the taxonomy's
    \textbf{verbose} label role. This is not an arbitrary choice: it is the \emph{exact} text EDINET's own
    CSV export (\S\ref{sec:edinet-oracle}) uses to name each fact --- verified byte-for-byte against a real
    filing (Toyota, E02144) at \textbf{106/106} narrative text-blocks and \textbf{338/338} numeric line
    items, versus \textbf{0/106} for the shorter \emph{standard} label role (which drops the disambiguating
    ``\jpcjk{、…}{}'' qualifier and the ``\jpcjk{［テキストブロック］}{}'' tag). So a fact names identically
    to the regulator's parse. Present for every \emph{live} concept.
  \item \texttt{type} (\texttt{monetaryItemType}, \texttt{textBlockItemType}, \ldots) --- so numeric line
    items and narrative sections are told apart. This is how the narrative text-blocks (\texttt{jpcrp\_cor})
    are \emph{found}: they are exactly the \texttt{textBlockItemType} concepts.
  \item \texttt{balance} (\texttt{debit} / \texttt{credit} / \texttt{null}) and \texttt{periodType} ---
    concept metadata. \textbf{\texttt{balance} is \emph{not} used to set or correct a value's sign.} An XBRL
    value is already canonically signed --- SG\&A is stored \(+1000\) because it is a debit expense, and
    that is exactly what \texttt{facts(f)} returns --- confirmed against the CSV oracle at \textbf{1378/1378}
    facts with \emph{zero} sign-flips (\S\ref{sec:edinet-oracle}). \texttt{balance} is carried for
    normalisation and consistency checks only, never as a sign rewrite.
\end{itemize}
A concept absent from the table is a filer's own \emph{extension} concept (e.g.\
\texttt{jpcrp030000-asr\_E02144-000:StrategyClimateChangeTextBlock}); its label and metadata come from the
filing's own bundled linkbase, not the standard vocabulary.

\paragraph{Where it lives.} Under the EDINET system directory, one file per taxonomy \emph{effective date}:
\begin{lstlisting}
src/filing_systems/edinet/data/edinet_concepts_2024-11-01.json   % 9,569 concepts
src/filing_systems/edinet/data/edinet_concepts_2025-11-01.json   % 9,596 concepts
\end{lstlisting}
\noindent Each is $\sim$2.8\,MB and keyed exactly as filings reference concepts (\texttt{prefix:LocalName}).
The distiller is a one-shot developer script (not shipped code); only its JSON output is committed.

\paragraph{What changes from one year to the next.} EDINET ships a new taxonomy version late each year
(the ``\(N{+}1\)'' package lands around \texttt{N-11-01}). The governing rule is that a taxonomy is
\textbf{append-with-deprecation, never mutate-in-place}: changing a live concept's meaning would silently
break every filing that ever used it, so instead the old concept is \emph{deprecated} (kept, flagged) and a
new one added. The practical consequence --- verified by diffing the \texttt{2024-11-01} and
\texttt{2025-11-01} tables --- is that \textbf{the newest table is a safe superset for all prior years}:
the sign of an existing concept never moves, and nothing is ever removed. Concretely, across the
\(9\,569\) concepts common to both years (Table~\ref{tab:edinet-taxdiff}):

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{The honest, non-overlapping tally: EDINET \texttt{2024-11-01} \(\to\) \texttt{2025-11-01} (verbose
labels). The buckets are \emph{disjoint}, so they sum to the \(48\) distinct concepts touched --- but only
the \(27\) additions move the total from \(9\,569\) to \(9\,596\). (The raw per-language label-change
counts, \(15\)~EN and \(21\)~JA, are deliberately \emph{not} shown as buckets: they double-count the \(7\)
deprecations, since deprecating a concept nulls its label in both languages.)}
\label{tab:edinet-taxdiff}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.46\textwidth}p{0.09\textwidth}p{0.33\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Bucket} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Affects the \(9\,569{\to}9\,596\) count?} \\
\midrule
added (new disclosures) & 27 & \textbf{yes} \((+27)\) --- e.g.\ \texttt{InterestExpensesOnLeaseLiabilitiesNOE} \\
live \(\to\) deprecated (balance kept) & 7 & no (still present) --- \texttt{RightOfUseAssets}, \texttt{SalesDiscountsNOE}, \ldots \\
genuine label rewordings & 14 & no --- cosmetic, e.g.\ a renumbered cross-reference in the verbose qualifier \\
\midrule
\textbf{distinct concepts changed} & \textbf{48} & \\
\texttt{balance} / \texttt{type} / \texttt{periodType} changed & \textbf{0} & --- (metadata is stable across versions) \\
concepts removed & \textbf{0} & --- (append-only; old filings never orphaned) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent A caveat that follows from the append-only rule: the 7 live\(\to\)deprecated concepts lose their
verbose label in the newer table (deprecation carries only a deprecation \emph{note}, not a name), though
they keep their \texttt{balance} and \texttt{type}. Naming is unaffected in practice --- the filing supplies
its own bundled label --- but it is why both years are kept rather than only the newest: the older table
still holds the labels for concepts the newer one has retired.

\section{Validation: the EDINET CSV oracle}
\label{sec:edinet-oracle}

Every system in this manual proves its numbers against an independent reference (\S\ref{sec:validation}):
the SEC path cross-checks \texttt{company\_facts}, and ESEF and Companies House cross-check
\href{https://filings.xbrl.org}{filings.xbrl.org}, the Arelle-backed XBRL-International aggregator.
\textbf{Japan has neither} --- \texttt{company\_facts} is SEC-only, and filings.xbrl.org does not carry
EDINET. Without an oracle, ``\texttt{facts(f)} ran and returned numbers'' is not evidence that the numbers
are \emph{right}: a wrong sign, a dropped scale factor, a mis-resolved period or a corrupted nil reads as
a perfectly ordinary value.

The escape is built into the EDINET document API itself, which serves the \emph{same} filing in two forms:

\begin{itemize}[nosep]
  \item \texttt{GET /documents/\{docID\}?type=1} \(\rightarrow\) the submission \textbf{ZIP} (inline XBRL +
    bundled taxonomy) --- the bytes \emph{we} parse with \texttt{facts(f)}.
  \item \texttt{GET /documents/\{docID\}?type=5} \(\rightarrow\) a \textbf{CSV} of the tagged facts ---
    \textbf{the FSA's own parse of that exact same document}.
\end{itemize}

\noindent So the regulator extracts the facts for us, from byte-identical source. The validation
(planned step \textbf{E4.2}) is therefore the strongest kind available: fetch both forms of one filing,
run \texttt{facts(f)} over the ZIP, and assert our values match the CSV's \textbf{row for row}. It is
\emph{same-document} (not a headline cross-check against a different data provider),
\emph{authoritative} (the regulator's own parser, the JP analogue of Arelle), \emph{free}, and
\emph{fact-level}. This is exactly the methodology that caught real extraction bugs on the SEC path ---
the \texttt{\&\#8212;} em-dash entity corrupting every nil fact into the value \texttt{8212}, and the
sign-flip on classic (non-inline) instances --- now turned on Japan. (The weaker checks still apply
alongside it: Yahoo Finance headline totals for Tokyo tickers, e.g.\ Toyota \texttt{7203.T}.) Because
\texttt{type=5} is authenticated, the oracle run waits on the same free Subscription-Key as live
discovery.

\section{Workflow Use Case Examples}\label{sec:edinet-workflows}

Practical EDINET workflows --- a few lines each. With the live API still pending a Subscription-Key, the
runnable example today is the offline parse-and-classify of a submission ZIP; live discovery and the
CSV-oracle validation follow once the key lands.

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> f = fetch_filing(EDINET(), "submission.zip")     # local submission ZIP, offline
julia> rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true); # jppfs_cor / jpigp_cor facts, classified
julia> select_statement(rows, "BalanceSheet")           # BS / IS / CF as usual
julia> label_map(f; lang = "en")                        # English labels (lang="ja" for Japanese)
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{subappendices}
\section{Credentials}\label{sec:edinet-credentials}

EDINET's REST API requires a \emph{free} \textbf{Subscription-Key}, obtained from the EDINET API
registration page. Set it per session, or from the environment:

\begin{lstlisting}
set_credentials(EDINET(); api_key = ENV["EDINET_API_KEY"])
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent Offline parsing of a submission ZIP needs no credential; only live discovery and the CSV oracle
(\S\ref{sec:edinet-oracle}) use the key.
\end{subappendices}

\chapter{South Korea: DART}
\label{sys:dart}

DART (and its API, OpenDART) is South Korea's FSS disclosure system, and the \textbf{fifth}
\texttt{FilingSystem}. A filing's financials are served as an XBRL \textbf{package} --- a ZIP of the
classic \texttt{.xbrl} instance plus its bundled linkbases --- so, like ESEF and EDINET, it parses
through the \emph{shared} report-package reader and the jurisdiction-agnostic XBRL extractor
(\S\ref{sys:esef}, \S\ref{sys:extraction}). Korea adopted full IFRS as \textbf{K-IFRS}, so the working
thesis is that DART filings classify against the \emph{existing} \texttt{ifrs-full} vocabulary with
\textbf{no new national GAAP} --- and, verified live, they do (\S\ref{sec:dart-kifrs}). Identity is the
eight-digit OpenDART corporate code (\texttt{EntityId(:corp\_code,\ "00126380")}) and/or the six-digit
KRX stock code (\texttt{:stock\_code}).

\emph{Status:} the system, the offline parse, the live API discovery/fetch, the K-IFRS classification and
the language-aware Korean/English labels are \textbf{implemented and verified live} (against Samsung
Electronics and SK~Hynix); independent validation uses the Yahoo Finance cross-check
(\S\ref{sec:dart-oracle}).

\paragraph{Formats.} DART does \emph{not} mirror EDINET's clean PDF/XBRL/CSV triple (\S\ref{sys:edinet}).
It exposes the financials as \textbf{classic XBRL} --- the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package is a separate
\texttt{.xbrl} instance plus its linkbases, \emph{not} inline XBRL --- and as a pre-parsed \textbf{JSON}
statement API (\texttt{fnlttSinglAcntAll}, the primary statements only --- the FSS's own parse,
\S\ref{sec:dart-oracle}). There is \textbf{no CSV} endpoint (no analogue of EDINET's \texttt{type=5}); the
disclosure document itself (\texttt{document.xml}) is human-readable DART markup, out of scope for
\texttt{facts()}.

\paragraph{No narratives from the XBRL.} \texttt{narratives(f)} (\S\ref{sec:api-narratives}) returns
\textbf{nothing} for a DART filing --- and that is correct, not a gap in the extractor. The reason: DART's
\texttt{fnlttXbrl} package is the \textbf{numeric financial statements only}; a DART instance carries
\emph{no} text-block facts (verified on a real filing --- Hyundai Motor's FY2025 report has \textbf{zero}
\texttt{TextBlock}/\texttt{Explanatory}-suffixed concepts). DART's narrative disclosures live in the separate
\texttt{document.xml} --- human-readable DART markup, \emph{not} XBRL-tagged (and out of scope, above) --- so
there is simply nothing for the XBRL-based \texttt{narratives()} to extract. Recovering DART prose would need
a \texttt{document.xml} parser, not the XBRL path. (Contrast EDINET, whose iXBRL tags text-blocks, and ESEF,
whose IFRS text-blocks use the \texttt{…Explanatory} suffix.)

\section{Entities covered}\label{sec:dart-entities}

DART receives disclosures from listed and other disclosure-obligated companies, plus funds and ownership
filers (Table~\ref{tab:dart-entities}). \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts the K-IFRS financial statements that the
periodic business reports carry. A filer is identified by its eight-digit OpenDART corp-code and, for listed
issuers, its six-digit KRX stock code.

\begin{table}[h]

\toprule
\textbf{Entity type} & \textbf{Example} & \textbf{Primary documents} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
Listed company (KOSPI / KOSDAQ) & Samsung (005930) & Business / Half-year / Quarterly Report & financial statements extracted \\
Other disclosure-obligated company & --- & Periodic reports (corp-code only) & extracted where an XBRL package exists \\

\subsection{Listed companies}
KOSPI- and KOSDAQ-listed corporations, identified by both an OpenDART corp-code (eight-digit) and a KRX
stock code (six-digit) --- e.g.\ Samsung Electronics (corp \texttt{00126380}, stock \texttt{005930}) and
SK~Hynix. They file the periodic business reports that carry the K-IFRS financial statements; extracted in
full.

\subsection{Other disclosure-obligated companies}
Non-listed companies subject to external-audit and registration disclosure, identified by corp-code only.
Their periodic reports carry K-IFRS statements through the same \texttt{fnlttXbrl} path and are extracted
wherever an XBRL package is available.

\subsection{Investment funds}
Collective investment vehicles, which file fund-specific reports. Discovery is reachable through OpenDART;
extraction is \emph{not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Large-holding and ownership filers}
Investors crossing the 5\,\% large-holding threshold, and executives and major shareholders reporting their
holdings. They file ownership disclosures rather than financial statements. Discovery only --- extraction is
\emph{not implemented yet}.

\section{Forms and statement types}\label{sec:dart-forms}

OpenDART groups disclosures into several families (Table~\ref{tab:dart-forms}); \texttt{EDGAR.jl} extracts
the periodic disclosures --- the carriers of the financial statements --- and reaches the rest for discovery
as the adapter matures. Each family is detailed below, followed by the statement types those reports carry.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{DART disclosure families and their support in \texttt{EDGAR.jl}.}
\label{tab:dart-forms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.43\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Family ($\to$ subsection)} & \textbf{Documents} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
Periodic disclosures & Business Report (annual), Half-year Report, Quarterly Reports & statements extracted \\
Major-event \& issuance & Major-Matters Report; securities registration statements & \emph{not yet} \\
Ownership disclosures & 5\,\% large-holding report; executive / major-shareholder holding report & \emph{not yet} \\
Other disclosures & fair-disclosure and other periodic filings & \emph{not yet} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{Periodic disclosures}
The business report (\krcjk{사업보고서}{\emph{saeop bogoseo}}, the annual report), the half-year report and the
quarterly reports --- DART's analogue of the 10-K/10-Q, carrying the K-IFRS financial statements. Extracted
in full via the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package.

\subsection{Major-event and issuance disclosures}
The major-matters report and securities registration statements, filed on material events and securities
offerings. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Ownership disclosures}
The 5\,\% large-holding report and the executive / major-shareholder holding report. \emph{Not implemented
yet}.

\subsection{Other disclosures}
Fair-disclosure and other periodic filings. \emph{Not implemented yet}.

\subsection{Statement types: K-IFRS by reuse}\label{sec:dart-kifrs}

DART is the case the two-axis design (\S\ref{sec:two-axes}) was built for: a new \emph{jurisdiction} that
brings \textbf{no new taxonomy}. Because K-IFRS \emph{is} IFRS, a Korean filer's primary statements are
tagged with bare \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts (\texttt{ifrs-full:Assets}, \texttt{ifrs-full:Revenue},
\texttt{ifrs-full:ProfitLoss}, \texttt{ifrs-full:CashFlowsFromUsedInOperatingActivities}, \ldots), which
the existing \texttt{ifrs-full} vocabulary (shared with ESEF, Companies House and EDINET's IFRS filers)
already classifies. \textbf{No \texttt{vocab\_krgaap.jl} was needed.}

This was confirmed on two real FY2023 annual reports (\texttt{fnlttXbrl} packages): Samsung Electronics
(\texttt{5{,}997} facts) and SK~Hynix (\texttt{6{,}306} facts). In both, roughly \textbf{80\,\% of
concepts are bare \texttt{ifrs-full}} (Samsung \texttt{4{,}735}, SK~Hynix \texttt{5{,}072}); the remainder
are the filer's own \texttt{entity\ldots} extensions ($\sim$11\,\%) and Korea-specific \texttt{dart} /
\texttt{dart-gcd} concepts ($\sim$10\,\%). The balance sheet, income statement and cash-flow statement all
classify through the shared vocabulary, and \texttt{select\_statement} round-trips on both filers (Samsung
BS~1757 / IS~409 / CF~227; SK~Hynix BS~2043 / IS~126 / CF~216); a filing flows through the same pipeline as
every other system (\S\ref{sec:dart-workflows}).

\noindent Even the Korea-specific lines classify without a vocabulary. \textbf{Operating income}
(\krcjk{영업이익}{\emph{yeongeop iik}}) is tagged \texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} --- a \texttt{dart}-namespace
concept, not \texttt{ifrs-full} --- yet \texttt{facts(f;\ classify=true)} still places it on the income
statement, because the classification engine reads the filing's \emph{presentation-linkbase statement
roles}, not concept anchors alone (\S\ref{sys:extraction}). So a national extension is classified by
\emph{where} it sits in the income-statement role, and no \texttt{vocab\_krgaap.jl} is needed at all ---
verified on Samsung, where \texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} and numerous other \texttt{dart}/\texttt{entity}
lines (e.g.\ cash-flow proceeds) are correctly bucketed. This is a stronger result than EDINET's, which
needed a \texttt{jpigp\_cor} vocabulary addition (\S\ref{sys:edinet}): for K-IFRS, reuse plus role-based
classification suffices outright.

\section{Discovery and the corp-code resolver}

Unlike EDINET (date-based), OpenDART discovery is \textbf{by company}, because DART publishes a clean
identity index. \texttt{discover(OpenDARTApi();\ corp\_code|stock\_code,\ bgn\_de,\ end\_de)} lists a
company's periodic disclosures from \texttt{list.json} and returns handles, keeping the annual business
report (\krcjk{사업보고서}{\emph{saeop bogoseo}}) and pointing each handle at the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} financial-statement package
(\texttt{reprt\_code\ =\ "11011"}). A free \texttt{crtfc\_key} is required, set via
\texttt{set\_credentials(DART();\ api\_key)} or the \texttt{DART\_API\_KEY} / \texttt{OPENDART\_API\_KEY}
environment variable. (OpenDART authenticates with the key as a \emph{query parameter}, not a header, so
unlike the other keyed systems \texttt{system\_headers(::DART)} is empty and the key is appended to the
URL.)

Every other OpenDART endpoint is keyed by the \texttt{corp\_code}, not the public ticker, so resolution
goes through \texttt{corpCode.xml} --- a ZIP holding one \texttt{CORPCODE.xml} that maps
\texttt{corp\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ stock\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ name} for \emph{every} registered
Korean entity ($\sim$118{,}000; $\sim$4{,}000 listed). \texttt{dart\_corp\_code("005930")} resolves a
ticker (or a name) to its \texttt{corp\_code}, parsing that index in a single linear pass
(\texttt{0.84\,s} for 118{,}371 rows) and caching it (\S\ref{sec:dart-caching}).

\paragraph{Bilingual labels.} DART's label linkbase is \emph{split per language} ---
\texttt{\ldots\_lab-ko.xml} (Korean) and \texttt{\ldots\_lab-en.xml} (English), each single-language ---
unlike EDINET's one bilingual file. So \texttt{label\_map(f;\ lang)} (and \texttt{facts(f;\ labels=true,\
lang)}) selects the file for the requested language: \texttt{lang="en"} yields English labels,
\texttt{lang="ko"} Korean. On Samsung this gives 1{,}742 labels in each language (e.g.\
\texttt{dart:OperatingIncomeLoss} \(\to\) ``Operating income(loss)'' / ``\krcjk{영업이익(손실)}{yeongeop iik (sonsil)}'').
The \texttt{lang} keyword threads through the shared linkbase fetcher but is consulted only by systems
with per-language label files (just DART today); every other system ignores it, so their behaviour is
unchanged.

\section{Caching: slow endpoints and the per-system policy}\label{sec:dart-caching}

OpenDART's \emph{document} endpoints are \textbf{pathologically slow} --- measured at $\sim$12\,KB/s, so a
single \texttt{fnlttXbrl} filing takes minutes and \texttt{corpCode.xml} (\texttt{3.4\,MB}) clocked
\textbf{293\,s} --- while its metadata \texttt{list.json} answers in $\sim$1\,s. This is the forcing case
for the \textbf{per-system cache policy} (\S\ref{sec:caching}): a single global cache mode is too coarse
when DART is minutes-slow but the SEC and ESEF are fast. \texttt{set\_cache\_policy(DART();\
cache\ =\ :persistent)} makes \emph{only} DART persist its downloads across sessions (each slow file paid
once, ever) while everything else keeps the ephemeral default. It is the recommended setting for any
sustained DART work.

The corp-code index has its own renewal rule, because the
\texttt{corp\_code\ $\leftrightarrow$\ stock\_code} mapping is stable: rather than the global 24\,h
\texttt{cache\_ttl} (which would re-pay the multi-minute download daily), it is rebuilt only when older
than a jurisdiction-specific backstop --- \textbf{30 days} by default, tunable with
\texttt{dart\_index\_ttl!(days)}. As an immediate self-heal for newly-listed companies, a \emph{ticker}
lookup that misses re-downloads the index once and retries; \texttt{dart\_corp\_code(q;\ refresh\ =\
true)} forces a fresh pull on demand.

\section{Validation: independent cross-checks}\label{sec:dart-oracle}

Korea is the hardest jurisdiction to validate independently. filings.xbrl.org does \textbf{not} carry KR,
so there is no Arelle xBRL-JSON oracle (unlike ESEF/UKSEF, \S\ref{sec:validation}), and
\texttt{company\_facts}/AlphaVantage are US-centric. The independent reference that \emph{does} cover Korea
is \textbf{Yahoo Finance}, whose KRX coverage uses the suffixes \texttt{.KS} (KOSPI) and \texttt{.KQ}
(KOSDAQ) --- e.g.\ Samsung \texttt{005930.KS}, SK~Hynix \texttt{000660.KS}. The do-it-yourself benchmark
helper \texttt{src/sources/yahoo\_oracle.jl} compares EDGAR.jl's revenue, net income, assets and equity for
the latest annual report against Yahoo's --- an \emph{independent data pipeline}, the same arms-length check it
applies to every other jurisdiction. One DART subtlety the wiring handles: the consolidated face line is
not left undimensioned but tagged on the \texttt{ConsolidatedAndSeparateFinancialStatementsAxis}
(\texttt{ConsolidatedMember}), so those face facts are normalised to undimensioned for the shared metric
picker.

Yahoo covers only headline totals, so the \textbf{statement of changes in equity} --- a movements-by-equity-component
table --- needs a fact-level, dimension-aware reference. That is the \textbf{local Arelle oracle}
(\texttt{src/sources/arelle\_local\_oracle.jl}, \S\ref{sec:validation}): it runs Arelle itself on the
downloaded \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package and compares \emph{every} fact, keyed by concept~+~period~+~full
dimension set, so the per-component SCE cells are checked individually. On Samsung FY2023 it confirms
\textbf{5997/5997} of EDGAR.jl's facts against Arelle --- all five statements, the SCE included
(159/159) --- comparing values \emph{raw} (Arelle reports the as-stored value; EDGAR.jl's classified value
flips sign for \texttt{negatedLabel}/cash-outflow concepts).

OpenDART additionally re-serves each filing's parsed accounts as \texttt{fnlttSinglAcntAll.json} (the FSS's
own parse). It is a handy sanity peek --- \texttt{account\_id} values such as \texttt{ifrs-full\_Assets} map
straight onto our concepts --- but because it comes from the \emph{same} disclosure system as the filing it
is not an independent reference, so EDGAR.jl ships no comparison harness for it.

\section{Workflow Use Case Examples}\label{sec:dart-workflows}

Practical DART workflows --- a few lines each. The live path resolves a KRX stock code to its corp-code,
fetches the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package, then classifies and labels through the shared pipeline; more
examples will follow.

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> set_credentials(DART(); api_key = ENV["DART_API_KEY"])    # free OpenDART crtfc_key
julia> hs = discover(OpenDARTApi(); stock_code = "005930",       # Samsung; resolves to corp_code 00126380
                     bgn_de = "20240101", end_de = "20241231");
julia> f  = fetch_filing(first(hs));                             # the fnlttXbrl package
julia> rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true);          # K-IFRS facts, classified
julia> select_statement(rows, "BalanceSheet")                    # BS / IS / CF as usual
julia> label_map(f; lang = "en")                                 # English labels (lang="ko" for Korean)
\end{lstlisting}

% ===========================================================================
% ===  VALIDATION  ==========================================================
% ===========================================================================
\begin{subappendices}
\section{Credentials}\label{sec:dart-credentials}

OpenDART requires a \emph{free} \texttt{crtfc\_key} (register on the OpenDART portal). Set it per session, or
from the environment:

\begin{lstlisting}
set_credentials(DART(); api_key = ENV["DART_API_KEY"])
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The same key authenticates discovery, the \texttt{fnlttXbrl} package download and the
\texttt{corpCode.xml} resolver.
\end{subappendices}

\chapter{China: CSRC and CNINFO}
\label{sys:china}

China is being added as the \textbf{seventh} \texttt{FilingSystem}, and it is unlike the six before it in
one decisive respect: it is the first \textbf{PDF-native} jurisdiction. Every prior system --- SEC, ESEF,
Companies House, EDINET, DART, India --- exposes an XBRL or iXBRL package as its keyless primary channel,
so \texttt{facts(f)} flows through the shared jurisdiction-agnostic XBRL extractor. China does not. Its only
complete, keyless, machine-accessible channel yields \textbf{PDF annual reports under CAS} --- so a China
