\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_RESULTIR\_} & REITs / InvITs & \texttt{RevenueFromOperations}, \texttt{NetDistributableCashFlows} & verified; bundled \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Insurers and REITs --- the \texttt{in-capmkt} Integrated Filing seam (handled).} The
\texttt{corporates-financial-results} feed above is the \emph{old} channel, frozen at $\sim$FY2024; SEBI's
current \textbf{Integrated Filing} framework serves \emph{every} sector on a \emph{different} taxonomy,
\textbf{\texttt{in-capmkt}} (\texttt{www.sebi.gov.in/xbrl/2025-01-31/in-capmkt}) --- including insurers and
REITs/InvITs, which the old feed never carried. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} handles it as a second discovery source,
\texttt{IntegratedFilings} (\S\ref{sec:india-retrieve}), over the \texttt{integrated-filing-results}
endpoint, with a dedicated \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} and bundled \texttt{in-capmkt} presentation/label/
calculation linkbases (one per sector). Life (\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_LI\_}) and general
(\texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_GI\_}) insurers report a premium-driven revenue account
(\texttt{GrossPremiumIncome} / \texttt{GrossPremiumsWritten}) with no \texttt{in-bse-fin} equivalent, verified
against SBI~Life, HDFC~Life and ICICI~Lombard. \textbf{REITs/InvITs} are the one sector that stayed on the
\emph{old} \texttt{corporates-financial-results} endpoint --- reached with \texttt{index=reitsinvits} (not the
integrated-filing endpoint, which returns nothing for them) --- but file the Integrated Filing taxonomy all
the same, as \texttt{INTEGRATED\_FILING\_RESULTIR\_} XBRL on \texttt{in-capmkt} \emph{2026-01-31}; verified
against Embassy~REIT and IndiGrid~InvIT. Their P\&L uses the general Ind-AS concepts
(\texttt{RevenueFromOperations}), so \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} already anchors it, with
\texttt{NetDistributableCashFlows} landing in the cash-flow statement.

\subsection{Taxonomies}\label{sec:india-taxonomies}
Each family's formats share a national namespace but at a \emph{version} that varies by template. Within
\texttt{in-bse-fin} the general Ind-AS template binds \texttt{.../2020-03-31/in-bse-fin}, the banking
template \texttt{.../2019-09-30/in-bse-fin}; within \texttt{in-capmkt} the sector templates bind
\texttt{.../2025-01-31/in-capmkt} (REITs/InvITs, \texttt{.../2026-01-31/in-capmkt}). So ``the Indian
taxonomy'' is really \emph{two} small families of BSE/SEBI-published templates, each sharing IFRS-identical
local names. Classification keys on the namespace --- not the version or the prefix --- so one
\texttt{vocab\_indas} spans every \texttt{in-bse-fin} format and one \texttt{vocab\_incapmkt} spans every
\texttt{in-capmkt} format (\S\ref{sec:india-classify}); but code that resolves labels or the calculation
linkbase must use the exact version \emph{and} sector the instance declares (the linkbases are bundled
per-format, routed by the filename prefix).

\subsection{Statement Types and Classification}\label{sec:india-statements}\label{sec:india-classify}
A results filing is a \textbf{condensed} statement set, \emph{not} the full annual accounts. In the general
(\texttt{INDAS\_}) format it carries the \textbf{balance sheet} (\texttt{Assets},
\texttt{EquityAndLiabilities}), the \textbf{statement of profit and loss} including \textbf{other
comprehensive income} (\texttt{RevenueFromOperations}\,\ldots\,\texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod},
\texttt{ComprehensiveIncomeForThePeriod}), the \textbf{cash-flow statement} (indirect method ---
\texttt{TypeOfCashFlowStatement} $=$ \texttt{"Cash Flow Indirect"}) and \textbf{segment} reporting. It does
\emph{not} carry a statement of changes in equity, nor the notes --- those, with the full statements, live
in the separate annual report (Reg.~34, a PDF, out of scope). The \textbf{banking} format instead reports
interest earned / expended, deposits and advances; the \textbf{NBFC} format, Ind-AS on the NBFC schedule.
Every filing self-declares its shape through metadata concepts: \texttt{NatureOfReportStandaloneConsolidated},
\texttt{ReportingQuarter}, \texttt{WhetherResultsAreAuditedOrUnaudited}, \texttt{TypeOfCashFlowStatement},
\texttt{LevelOfRoundingUsedInFinancialStatements} (e.g.\ \texttt{"Crores"}) and
\texttt{DescriptionOfPresentationCurrency} (e.g.\ \texttt{"INR"}). An \emph{annual} results filing carries
both the current-quarter and the full-year figures, so \texttt{facts(f)} returns a 3-month \emph{and} a
12-month fact for each flow concept (just as an SEC 10-Q carries the quarter and the year-to-date); select
the one you want by its period, exactly as in any other jurisdiction.

\medskip\noindent Every format tags under a national namespace with IFRS-identical local names ---
\texttt{in-bse-fin} for the old feed, \texttt{in-capmkt} for Integrated Filing (\S\ref{sec:india-taxonomy}).
Because \texttt{EDGAR.jl} classifies by \emph{namespace}, not prefix, India is classified through two
dedicated national vocabularies --- \texttt{vocab\_indas.jl} keyed on \texttt{in-bse-fin} and
\texttt{vocab\_incapmkt.jl} keyed on \texttt{in-capmkt} --- the inverse of DART, which needed no vocabulary
at all (\S\ref{sec:dart-kifrs}). Each supplies the key-anchor concepts per format (the general Ind-AS lines,
the banking interest-based lines \emph{and} the insurers' premium lines); for the \emph{full} statement ---
every line item, not just the anchors --- \texttt{EDGAR.jl} bundles the BSE/SEBI \textbf{presentation
linkbase} (one per format, selected by the filename prefix), since the instances carry none and neither BSE
nor SEBI hosts them resolvably. Together they place the whole balance sheet, income statement and cash-flow
statement (verified: Reliance 243 of 294 facts classified, the rest being segment/dimensional). Two Ind-AS
presentation choices in the \emph{general}-format income statement --- revenue reported \emph{gross of
excise duty}, and a broad \emph{finance-costs} bucket --- are where our figures legitimately differ from
third-party sources; \S\ref{sec:india-yahoo} is the reconciliation map (for the general format; banks reconcile on
different lines).

\section{Retrieving Filings}\label{sec:india-retrieve}

Discovery is \textbf{keyless}. \texttt{discover(NSEFilings();\ symbol=\ldots)} lists a listed company's
financial-results filings by its NSE ticker; each returned \texttt{FilingHandle} carries the Ind-AS XBRL URL,
which \texttt{fetch\_filing} downloads into the same \texttt{Filing} every other system produces. The whole
flow is three lines:

\begin{lstlisting}
hs = discover(NSEFilings(); symbol = "RELIANCE")   # annual, consolidated, audited (the defaults)
f  = fetch_filing(first(hs))                        # newest filing; downloads the Ind-AS XBRL
facts(f)                                            # the statements  (narratives(f) for the Reg. 30 notes)
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent A few notes:

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{No key, no setup.} NSE's public JSON API sits behind bot protection that requires a browser
    session; \texttt{EDGAR.jl} bootstraps and refreshes that session automatically, so there is nothing to
    configure --- unlike EDINET, Companies House or DART, India needs no credential.
  \item \textbf{By ticker.} Discovery keys off the NSE \texttt{symbol} (e.g.\ \texttt{"RELIANCE"},
    \texttt{"TCS"}). The narrowing keywords are \texttt{period} (\texttt{"Annual"} or \texttt{"Quarterly"})
    and \texttt{consolidated} / \texttt{audited} (\texttt{true} / \texttt{false}, or \texttt{nothing} to
    keep both); the defaults return the annual, consolidated, audited filings.
  \item \textbf{Handles, newest first.} Each handle's identity is the filing's \textbf{ISIN}
    (\texttt{EntityId(:isin,\ "INE002A01018")}) and its \texttt{period\_end} the financial-year end, so
    \texttt{first(hs)} is the most recent; pick any other by period.
  \item \textbf{Same API as everywhere.} \texttt{fetch\_filing(h)} $\to$ \texttt{facts(f)} /
    \texttt{narratives(f)} / \texttt{facts(f;\ classify=true)} $\to$ \texttt{select\_statement} is identical
    to SEC, ESEF or DART --- nothing about working an Indian filing differs for the caller. Classification
    uses the dedicated \texttt{vocab\_indas} plus the bundled BSE presentation linkbase
    (\S\ref{sec:india-statements}).
\end{itemize}

\section{Reconciling Income-Statement Figures with Yahoo}\label{sec:india-yahoo}

India has no Arelle / \href{https://filings.xbrl.org}{filings.xbrl.org} oracle (the same gap as Korea,
\S\ref{sec:dart-oracle}), so the cheap independent cross-check on a filer's headline numbers is
\textbf{Yahoo Finance} (NSE tickers \texttt{.NS}, BSE \texttt{.BO}). But Yahoo's line-item \emph{names}, and
their \emph{scope}, are not the Ind-AS \texttt{in-bse-fin} concepts: a na\"ive value-against-value
comparison appears to ``fail'' on lines that are merely \emph{defined} differently, not mis-extracted. This
section is the map --- for each income-statement line, the XBRL concept, the Yahoo field, and how to get
from one to the other. It was verified line-by-line on three FY2024 consolidated filings: Reliance
Industries, Infosys and Indian~Oil. All three are \textbf{general-format} (\texttt{INDAS\_}) filers
(\S\ref{sec:india-forms}); a bank's \texttt{BANKING\_} results reconcile on different lines (interest
earned/expended, not revenue) and are not covered here.

\paragraph{Rule of thumb.} \textbf{Every profit line from profit-before-tax downward matches Yahoo to the
rupee} (tax, profit for the period, owners' share, minority interest --- and depreciation). The two lines
that carry Indian-GAAP-specific gross-ups --- \textbf{revenue} (excise duty) and \textbf{finance costs}
(lease interest, unwinding of discount) --- are where the two sources legitimately diverge. Anchor a
validation on the profit lines; treat revenue and finance-costs as \emph{definitional-difference} lines.

\subsection{The Concept Map}
Each income-statement line, as \texttt{in-bse-fin:} concept $\leftrightarrow$ Yahoo field (prefixes dropped):

\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Revenue:} \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} vs Yahoo \texttt{TotalRevenue} ---
    \emph{Yahoo $=$ XBRL $-$ excise duty}; equal when there is no excise (deep dive below).
  \item \textbf{Finance / interest cost:} \texttt{FinanceCosts} vs Yahoo \texttt{InterestExpense} --- the
    XBRL bucket is a \emph{superset} (adds lease-liability interest and unwinding of discount), so
    XBRL~$\ge$~Yahoo (deep dive below).
  \item \textbf{Depreciation:} \texttt{DepreciationDepletionAndAmortisationExpense} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{ReconciledDepreciation} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Profit before tax:} \texttt{ProfitBeforeTax} vs Yahoo \texttt{PretaxIncome} --- Yahoo may
    take \texttt{SegmentProfitBeforeTax}, which \emph{adds share of profit of associates/JVs} (Ind-AS shows
    that post-tax): \texttt{PretaxIncome $=$ ProfitBeforeTax $+$ ShareOfProfit\ldots Associates}.
  \item \textbf{Tax:} \texttt{TaxExpense} vs Yahoo \texttt{TaxProvision} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Profit for the period:} \texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{NetIncomeContinuousOperations} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Net income (owners):} \texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToOwnersOfParent} vs Yahoo
    \texttt{NetIncome} / \texttt{NetIncomeCommonStockholders} --- \emph{equal}.
  \item \textbf{Non-controlling interest:} \texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToNonControllingInterests} vs
    Yahoo \texttt{MinorityInterests} --- \emph{equal} (Yahoo carries it negative).
  \item \textbf{Basic EPS:} \texttt{BasicEarningsLossPerShareFromContinuingAndDiscontinuedOperations} vs
    Yahoo \texttt{BasicEPS} --- Yahoo \emph{restates for later bonus issues / splits} (Reliance
    \texttt{102.90} $\to$ Yahoo \texttt{51.45} after the 1:1 bonus).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Revenue --- Excise Duty}
Ind-AS reports \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} \textbf{gross of excise duty}, with the excise shown as a
separate expense line --- tagged \texttt{in-bse-fin:OtherExpenses} in the operating-expense breakdown member
whose \texttt{DescriptionOfOtherExpenses} is \texttt{"Excise Duty"}. Yahoo (like most data vendors) presents
revenue \textbf{net of excise}. Because the excise sits in \emph{both} revenue and expenses, it cancels at
profit-before-tax --- which is why every profit line still matches. Post-GST (2017) excise survives only on
\textbf{petroleum} (petrol, diesel, ATF, crude, still outside GST), so this affects refiners and not
services or most goods companies. Table~\ref{tab:india-rev-recon} works it across the three filers.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Revenue reconciliation, FY2024 (Rs crore). \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} $-$ excise duty
($-$ other operating revenue) $=$ Yahoo \texttt{TotalRevenue}.}
\label{tab:india-rev-recon}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.22\textwidth}rrrr@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Filer} & \textbf{Rev.\ from ops} & \textbf{$-$ Excise} & \textbf{$-$ Other op.\ rev.} & \textbf{$=$ Yahoo} \\
\midrule
Reliance Industries & 914{,}472 & 13{,}408 & --- & 901{,}064 \\
Indian Oil (IOC)    & 881{,}235 & 104{,}884 & $\approx$3{,}510 & 772{,}842 \\
Infosys             & 153{,}670 & --- (none) & --- & 153{,}674$^{*}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent For Reliance the excise line closes the gap \textbf{exactly} ($914{,}472 - 13{,}408 = 901{,}064$).
For Infosys (IT services) there is \emph{no} excise, so revenue matches. Indian~Oil is the honest edge case:
excise ($104{,}884$) explains 96.8\,\% of the gap, leaving a $\sim$Rs~3{,}510~crore residual that is
\textbf{not present anywhere in the condensed results XBRL} (its \texttt{RevenueFromOperations} is a single
undivided line). The most likely source is \emph{other operating revenue} netted out by the vendor but
itemised only in the full annual report --- a reminder that for a \emph{rupee-exact} revenue check the
quarterly results filing is sometimes insufficient, and one should anchor on profit-before-tax (excise- and
associates-neutral) instead. \footnotesize$^{*}$Yahoo reports Infosys in \textbf{USD} (\$18.562~bn); see
\emph{Caveats}.\normalsize

\subsection{Finance Costs --- a broader bucket than Yahoo's interest expense}
Ind-AS \texttt{FinanceCosts} is a \emph{superset} of Yahoo's \texttt{InterestExpense}: besides interest on
borrowings it includes interest on \textbf{lease liabilities} (Ind-AS~116), \textbf{unwinding of discount}
on provisions (decommissioning / asset-retirement) and other borrowing costs, whereas Yahoo's
\texttt{InterestExpense} is the narrower pure-debt figure. So the XBRL value is consistently $\ge$ Yahoo's:
Reliance \texttt{23{,}118} vs \texttt{21{,}772}; Indian~Oil \texttt{7{,}826} vs \texttt{7{,}584} (Rs~crore).
It is a scope difference, not an error.

\subsection{The lines that match, and the associates reclassification}
Tax (\texttt{TaxExpense} $=$ \texttt{TaxProvision}), profit for the period (\texttt{ProfitLossForPeriod} $=$
\texttt{NetIncomeContinuousOperations}), owners' net income
(\texttt{ProfitOrLossAttributableToOwnersOfParent} $=$ \texttt{NetIncome}), minority interest and
depreciation all match \textbf{to the rupee} across the filers. The one apparent exception, profit-before-tax,
is a \emph{classification} difference: Indian~Oil's \texttt{ProfitBeforeTax} is \texttt{55{,}742} but Yahoo's
\texttt{PretaxIncome} is \texttt{57{,}288} --- because Yahoo takes \texttt{SegmentProfitBeforeTax}, which
includes the Rs~1{,}546~crore \emph{share of profit of associates} that Ind-AS presents \emph{after} tax.
It reconciles ($55{,}742 + 1{,}546 = 57{,}288$) and washes out by net income.

\subsection{Two caveats: currency and per-share restatement}
\textbf{Currency.} Yahoo does \emph{not} always report an Indian issuer in rupees. Companies that publish
USD financial statements are carried by Yahoo in \textbf{USD} --- Infosys's FY2024 income statement comes
back as \$18.562~bn revenue, \$3.167~bn net income --- so the currency must be \emph{detected}, not assumed
INR, and converted at the year's average rate (Infosys FY2024 $\approx$ Rs~82.79/USD, a rate the
depreciation line confirms: \$565~m $\times$ 82.79 $=$ Rs~4{,}678~crore, matching our figure exactly).
Reliance and Indian~Oil are reported in INR. \textbf{Per-share restatement.} Yahoo restates EPS for
\emph{later} bonus issues and splits, so a filed \texttt{BasicEarningsLossPerShare} will not match Yahoo's
\texttt{BasicEPS} across a corporate action (Reliance's filed \texttt{102.90} vs Yahoo's \texttt{51.45}
after the 2024 1:1 bonus).

\chapter{Russia: MOEX, CBR and e-Disclosure}
\label{sys:russia}

Russia is being added as the \textbf{ninth} \texttt{FilingSystem}, and it breaks a pattern every prior
jurisdiction shared: there is \emph{no single door}. SEC, ESEF, CNINFO, HKEXnews, NSE --- each earlier system
had one keyless portal spanning its whole reachable universe. Russia instead spreads corporate disclosure
across \emph{four} unrelated channels --- five authorised e-disclosure agencies (which mirror the same
issuer filings), the \textbf{Moscow Exchange} (MOEX, for listing and the securities master), the \textbf{Bank
of Russia} (CBR, the financial-sector regulator), and the tax-service corporate-registry channel behind the
\textbf{FNS} --- and the distinguishing problem is not \emph{which} door but \emph{access}: the canonical
primary agency is anti-bot-gated, so identity and facts must be assembled from the doors that are open.

Issuers report under \textbf{\rus{РСБУ}} (RAS, Russian Accounting Standards --- statutory, unconsolidated)
and, for large groups, \textbf{\rus{МСФО}} (IFRS, consolidated). Two open channels carry the facts:
\texttt{EDGAR.jl}'s Russian source is the \textbf{RFSD} --- an open, permissively-licensed derived dataset of
the \emph{entire non-financial universe's} RAS statements (\S\ref{sec:russia-forms}) --- with the CBR's public
\emph{bank} reporting covering the financial sector the RFSD excludes; \textbf{MOEX} supplies identity for
both. The gated primary agency (\texttt{e-disclosure.ru}) and the financial-sector XBRL are the harder,
less-open channels.

\section{Repositories}\label{sec:russia-repos}

Russia has no ``Russian EDGAR''. The legally-designated disclosure venues are \textbf{five authorised
information-disclosure agencies} (\rus{уполномоченные агентства}) that each republish the \emph{same}
mandated issuer filings --- the \rus{годовой отчёт} (annual report), accounting statements, consolidated
IFRS, and material-fact notices. Because they mirror one another, the agency that matters is simply
\emph{whichever one is reachable}: the canonical primary, Interfax's \texttt{e-disclosure.ru}, sits behind a
ServicePipe JavaScript challenge (a Russian anti-DDoS gate whose cookie must be computed by executing
obfuscated JS --- not solvable by a plain HTTP client), whereas two of the mirrors (\textbf{AK\&M} and
\textbf{PRIME}) are ungated. Alongside the agencies, \textbf{MOEX} runs an open securities-master API,
\textbf{CBR} publishes the financial sector's reporting, and the \textbf{FNS} corporate-registry channel ---
surfaced most usefully through the derived \textbf{RFSD} dataset --- carries the RAS statements of the whole
non-financial economy (Table~\ref{tab:russia-repos}).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Russian disclosure channels and their access model (probed from the dev environment). \texttt{EDGAR.jl}
assembles identity from MOEX and facts from RFSD (route~1) and CBR bank forms (route~2).}
\label{tab:russia-repos}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.20\textwidth}p{0.32\textwidth}p{0.20\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Channel} & \textbf{What it holds} & \textbf{Access} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
\textbf{e-disclosure.ru} (Interfax) & \emph{Primary} agency: every issuer's annual report, RAS \& IFRS
  statements, material facts --- as Cyrillic PDF & \textbf{Gated} --- ServicePipe JS-challenge (no plain-HTTP
  access) & not targeted (use a mirror) \\
\textbf{Mirror agencies} --- AK\&M (\texttt{disclosure.ru}), PRIME, SKRIN, AZIPI & The \emph{same} mandated
  filings, republished (legal mirrors) & AK\&M / PRIME \textbf{ungated}; SKRIN / AZIPI down at probe time &
  document fallback (deferred) \\
\textbf{MOEX ISS} (\texttt{iss.moex.com}) & Listing + securities master: \texttt{secid}, ISIN,
  \texttt{emitent\_id} and \textbf{INN} (the universal join key) & \textbf{Open}, keyless JSON &
  \textbf{identity resolver} \\
\textbf{CBR} (\texttt{cbr.ru}) & Financial-sector regulator: bank Forms 0409101/102 (public, per-\texttt{regnum});
  NFO XBRL taxonomy (public) & ddos-guard cookie (replayable); NFO \emph{instances} not public &
  \textbf{route~2} (banks) \\
\textbf{RFSD} (\texttt{irlspbru/RFSD}) & Derived firm-level dataset --- the \emph{non-financial universe's}
  RAS statements, 2011--2024, keyed by INN & \textbf{Open}, CC-BY-4.0, Parquet on HuggingFace &
  \textbf{route~1} (primary facts) \\
\textbf{FNS GIR BO} (\texttt{bo.nalog.ru}) & The authoritative per-firm RAS source \emph{behind} RFSD (XML per
  firm) & Fee-based API & upstream of RFSD --- not targeted \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The e-disclosure agencies --- one filing, five mirrors, one gate.} Russian securities law routes
issuer disclosure through five accredited agencies, each of which must republish the filings sent to it; the
sets are legally identical, so any \emph{one} reachable agency is a complete document source. The canonical
primary is Interfax's \texttt{e-disclosure.ru} --- the closest analogue to HKEXnews or EDGAR --- but it is
fronted by \textbf{ServicePipe}, a Russian anti-DDoS layer that returns a JavaScript challenge (\texttt{spsn}
/ \texttt{spid} cookies computed by an obfuscated script) rather than the page; a plain HTTP client is turned
away. Two of the mirrors are not gated: \textbf{AK\&M} (\texttt{disclosure.ru}, plain Apache/PHP) and
\textbf{PRIME} (\texttt{disclosure.1prime.ru}, ASP.NET), while \textbf{SKRIN} and \textbf{AZIPI} were
unreachable (503 / empty) at probe time. So the primary-source document path exists but is a fallback: it is
not needed for route~1, whose facts come structured from RFSD.

\paragraph{MOEX --- the open identity resolver.} The Moscow Exchange's Informational \& Statistical Server
(\texttt{iss.moex.com}) is fully \textbf{open and keyless}: \texttt{/iss/securities.json?q=Sberbank} returns
a clean record with \texttt{secid} (\texttt{SBER}), ISIN, \texttt{emitent\_id}, and --- decisively ---
\texttt{emitent\_inn}, the taxpayer number that is Russia's universal join key. MOEX plays the role NSE played
for India: it does not hold the accounts, but it turns a name or ticker into the INN that keys every other
channel (RFSD rows, CBR bank cards). This is the identity layer for both routes.

\paragraph{CBR --- the financial sector, and a taxonomy without public instances.} The Bank of Russia
(\texttt{cbr.ru}, behind the lighter \texttt{ddos-guard}, whose cookie is set inline and replayable) is the
\emph{only} door to financial-sector accounts, which the FNS/RFSD channel excludes by law. Its public
disclosure splits awkwardly: \textbf{banks} publish per-institution reporting (Forms 0409101/102),
addressable by registration number, but under the \emph{pre-XBRL} form regime; \textbf{non-credit financial
organisations} (insurers, pension funds, brokers) are the actual XBRL filers, but their filed \emph{instances}
are supervisory --- only the \textbf{taxonomy} is published openly (versioned ZIPs). The consequence, detailed
in \S\ref{sec:russia-entities}, is that CBR's public XBRL and CBR's public per-entity data do not overlap; the
buildable path is the bank forms (route~2).

\paragraph{FNS GIR BO and Rosstat --- the source behind RFSD.} Every Russian firm files its statutory RAS
accounts to the Federal Tax Service; these become public through the GIR BO resource (\texttt{bo.nalog.ru},
per-firm XML via a fee-based API) and, historically, Rosstat's open annual CSV dumps (2012--2018). Rather than
integrate that fee-gated API directly, \texttt{EDGAR.jl} consumes the \textbf{RFSD} --- an academic
re-packaging of exactly this channel into clean, partitioned Parquet (\S\ref{sec:russia-forms}) --- as route~1.

\section{Entities Covered}\label{sec:russia-entities}

Unlike Hong~Kong or China --- essentially one listed universe reached through one portal --- Russia's
reachable universe is \textbf{segmented by repository}, and the segments barely overlap
(Table~\ref{tab:russia-entities}). The largest is the \emph{non-financial economy}: every firm that files
statutory RAS accounts to the FNS, of order \textbf{2.25~million} firms per year, reached in bulk through
RFSD. A far smaller \emph{listed} slice (MOEX equities and bonds) is where market context and IFRS
consolidation live, and is reached for \emph{identity} through MOEX. The \emph{financial sector} --- banks and
non-credit financial organisations --- is carved out of the RAS universe entirely and reachable only through
CBR, and only in part.

\paragraph{The universe is not the data.} RFSD spans of order \textbf{9.6~million} firms across 2011--2024
($\sim$\textbf{56.6~million} firm-year observations), but \emph{eligibility} to file is not the same as
\emph{having} filed: only about \textbf{44\%} of eligible firm-years actually carry a filed statement (rising
to $\sim$71\% by 2023) --- the rest are eligible \emph{non-filers}, present as rows but empty, marked
\texttt{filed = 0} (Bondarkov, Ledenev, and Skougarevskiy (2025), App.~\ref{ch:references}). A
\emph{population} query therefore defaults to \texttt{filed = 1} (\S\ref{sec:russia-axes}); a \emph{point}
fetch of a non-filer returns a \texttt{Filing} with no facts.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Russia's reachable universe, segmented by repository (counts order-of-magnitude). The segments are
largely disjoint --- financials are excluded from the RAS/RFSD universe by law.}
\label{tab:russia-entities}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.22\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.18\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Segment} & \textbf{Inclusion criterion} & \textbf{Universe (order of mag.)} & \textbf{In
\texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Non-financial firms} & Every firm filing statutory RAS accounts to the FNS (incl.\ state-owned
  \emph{commercial} firms) & $\sim$2.25M / year & \textbf{route~1} via RFSD \\
\textbf{MOEX-listed issuers} & Companies with listed shares or bonds & $\sim$200 share issuers, hundreds more
  bond issuers & \textbf{identity} via MOEX; facts via RFSD (non-fin.) \\
\textbf{Credit institutions} (banks) & Licensed by the CBR & $\sim$320 (declining) & \textbf{route~2} via CBR
  Forms 101/102 \\
\textbf{Non-credit financial orgs} (insurers, NPFs, brokers) & CBR-regulated XBRL filers & insurers
  $\sim$130, NPFs $\sim$40, plus brokers/MFOs & deferred (instances not public) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{By standard: RAS unconsolidated vs IFRS consolidated}
A Russian issuer typically files \emph{two} distinct sets of accounts. The \textbf{\rus{РСБУ}} (RAS) statements
are \emph{statutory} and \emph{unconsolidated} --- the legal-entity accounts every firm must file to the FNS,
on a fixed national form (\S\ref{sec:russia-forms}); this is what RFSD collects. The \textbf{\rus{МСФО}} (IFRS)
statements are \emph{consolidated} and filed only by large groups (listed issuers, and any group above the
statutory thresholds), disclosed as PDF through the e-disclosure agencies, sometimes bilingually with an
English column. The two differ materially --- a holding company's unconsolidated RAS parent looks nothing like
its consolidated IFRS group --- so route~1 (RFSD/RAS) delivers the \emph{legal-entity} view, and the
consolidated IFRS view is a later, agency-sourced PDF extension for the listed subset.

\subsection{The financial-sector gap}
The single most important coverage fact is a \emph{carve-out}: firms ``ineligible to submit financial
statements to the Rosstat / Federal Tax Service by law --- financial, religious, or state organisations'' are
absent from the RAS/FNS channel, and therefore from RFSD. Banks and insurers instead report to the CBR. But
CBR's public data is itself split (\S\ref{sec:russia-repos}): banks are public but pre-XBRL (Forms 101/102),
while the true XBRL filers' instances are not published. So the financial sector is reachable only through the
bank-forms route, and only for credit institutions --- the cleanest reason to sequence RFSD first and CBR
second.

\subsection{Banks --- the CBR channel (route 2)}\label{sec:russia-banks}
The financial sector that route 1 excludes is reached through the \textbf{Bank of Russia} (CBR), and of it
only \textbf{credit institutions} (banks, $\sim$320) are publicly reachable \emph{per entity}. Each publishes
its statutory reporting as CBR \textbf{Form 0409101} (turnover balance by account) and \textbf{0409102}
(financial results), served as HTML by registration number (\texttt{regnum}) and reporting date --- the
\textbf{route-2} channel (\texttt{CBRBankFilings}, \S\ref{sec:russia-repos}). These are \emph{not} RFSD-style
structured rows and \emph{not} XBRL: they sit on the bank chart of accounts, so route~2 has its own fetch,
extractor and account\,$\to$\,statement map, and its own statement model (a bank has no revenue/COGS ---
interest income, provisions, account-level assets and liabilities). \textbf{Insurers and non-state pension
funds are \emph{not} available}: they report to the CBR in XBRL whose per-entity instances are not published
(the CBR releases only aggregate sector statistics), and --- unlike banks --- their per-entity accounts are
not served in HTML. So route~2 covers banks only; the remainder of the financial sector stays out of reach.

\subsection{Non-profit organisations (\rus{НКО})}
A large slice of the RFSD non-financial universe --- of order \textbf{130{,}000} filers in 2023 --- is
\textbf{non-profit organisations} (\rus{НКО}): foundations and charities, associations and unions, consumer
cooperatives, homeowners' associations (\rus{ТСЖ}), libraries, museums and the like (\emph{excluding} the
religious and state organisations the carve-out above removes). Having no revenue or profit, in place of the
income statement they file a \emph{dedicated} statement --- the \textbf{report on the targeted use of funds}
(OKUD 0710006): funds received (entrance and membership fees, voluntary donations, grants) against funds used
(targeted programmes, administrative upkeep, acquisitions). \texttt{EDGAR.jl} carries this as a first-class
statement, \texttt{TargetedFunds}, fetched and classified exactly like the other four (\S\ref{sec:russia-forms});
\texttt{select\_statement(rows, "TargetedFunds")} returns it. For a commercial filer the same lines are simply
zero.

\subsection{Identity: the INN key}
Every segment is keyed by the \textbf{INN} (\rus{ИНН}, the taxpayer identification number). MOEX ISS exposes it
(\texttt{emitent\_inn}) for any listed name; RFSD is keyed by it; CBR bank cards resolve from it via the
registration number. A single resolver --- name or ticker $\to$ INN through MOEX --- therefore threads all the
open channels, exactly as the five-digit stock code did for Hong~Kong and the NSE symbol did for India.

\subsection{Query axes --- RFSD is a population, not a document}\label{sec:russia-axes}
Because RFSD is a firm-level \emph{panel} --- of order \textbf{56.6M firm-year observations} across
2011--2024, one row per \texttt{(inn, year)} --- it is queryable on far more than firm identity. Every prior
\texttt{FilingSystem} is a \emph{document-fetch} system: it reaches a \emph{named entity} and nothing else.
RFSD supports that \textbf{point query} (one INN $\to$ that firm's firm-year rows, the \texttt{FilingSystem}
fetch path) \emph{and} a \textbf{population query} --- a cohort selected by industry, region, legal form,
ownership or age, crossed with the year partition. The selectable axes are the dataset's own \emph{dimension}
columns (Table~\ref{tab:russia-axes}); the 187 \texttt{line\_XXXX} columns are the \emph{measures} read off
whichever population the axes define.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{RFSD query axes (the dimension columns); the \texttt{line\_XXXX} columns are the measures. A
\emph{point} query fixes \texttt{inn}; a \emph{population} query fixes one or more of the rest, crossed with
\texttt{year}. All but firm/period are Russian \textbf{OK-classifiers} --- national code systems --- so a
sector or ownership query is a code match, not free text.}
\label{tab:russia-axes}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.17\textwidth}p{0.31\textwidth}p{0.40\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Axis} & \textbf{RFSD column(s)} & \textbf{Selects} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Firm} (point) & \texttt{inn} (primary), \texttt{ogrn}, \texttt{okpo} & one firm's firm-year rows ---
  the fetch path; INN preferred (the FNS / MOEX key) \\
\textbf{Period} & \texttt{year} (partition), \texttt{creation\_date}, \texttt{dissolution\_date},
  \texttt{age} & reporting year; firm-age cohorts and lifecycle \\
\textbf{Industry} & \texttt{okved}, \texttt{okved\_section} & firms in an economic-activity class (OKVED,
  NACE~Rev.2-compatible); \texttt{\_section} = coarse grouping \\
\textbf{Legal form} & \texttt{okopf} & organizational-legal form (LLC / JSC / PJSC \ldots) \\
\textbf{Ownership} & \texttt{okfc}, \texttt{okogu} & form of ownership (state / private / foreign / mixed);
  state-authority type (OKOGU) \\
\textbf{Geography} & \texttt{region}, \texttt{region\_taxcode}, \texttt{oktmo}, \texttt{lon}/\texttt{lat} &
  oblast, tax-region, municipality (OKTMO), or geocoded point \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\noindent The \textbf{quality flags} are query \emph{predicates}, not axes: a sound population is
\texttt{filed \& eligible \& !outlier}, preferring \texttt{articulated} rows (whose lines reconcile to their
subtotals), with \texttt{imputed} (reconstructed from adjacent years) and \texttt{simplified} (the abbreviated
KND~0710096 form) surfaced as provenance, and \texttt{financial} used only to confirm the sector's absence.
This population capability is what makes RFSD more than a RAS back-end: it turns Russia into the first
jurisdiction where \texttt{EDGAR.jl} can select an \emph{economy} --- a sector, a region, an ownership class
--- and not only a single filer.

\section{Forms and Statement Types}\label{sec:russia-forms}

What a Russian firm files, and in which format, follows the RAS/IFRS split. The statutory \textbf{RAS} accounts
use a \emph{fixed national form} with numbered lines --- the OKUD templates --- which is what makes route~1
tractable without any PDF extraction or national-language vocabulary: the line \emph{numbers} are the
concepts. Table~\ref{tab:russia-forms} maps the forms to their formats and to how \texttt{EDGAR.jl} reaches
them.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Russian statement types by standard, format, and route. Route~1 consumes the RAS forms as structured
Parquet; the rest are extensions.}
\label{tab:russia-forms}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.26\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.16\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Statement} & \textbf{Form / codes} & \textbf{Format} & \textbf{In \texttt{EDGAR.jl}} \\
\midrule
\textbf{RAS balance sheet} & OKUD \textbf{0710001} --- fixed lines (\texttt{line\_1600} total assets,
  \texttt{line\_1700} total liabilities \& equity) & \textbf{Parquet} (RFSD); XML (GIR BO); PDF (agencies) &
  \textbf{route~1} \\
\textbf{RAS income statement} & OKUD \textbf{0710002} --- fixed lines (\texttt{line\_2110} revenue,
  \texttt{line\_2400} net profit) & Parquet (RFSD); XML; PDF & \textbf{route~1} \\
\textbf{RAS cash flow / equity} & OKUD \textbf{0710004} / \textbf{0710003} & Parquet (RFSD, \texttt{CF*} /
  equity lines) & route~1 (subset) \\
\textbf{IFRS consolidated} (\rus{МСФО}) & Full IFRS statement set, \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts & \textbf{PDF}
  (agencies; sometimes bilingual) & extension (agency PDF) \\
\textbf{Bank reporting} & CBR Forms \textbf{0409101} (turnover balance by account) / \textbf{0409102}
  (financial results) & HTML / XLS (per \texttt{regnum}) & \textbf{route~2} \\
\textbf{NFO XBRL} & CBR taxonomy (insurers, NPFs, \ldots) & XBRL (taxonomy public; \emph{instances} not) &
  deferred \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{RFSD --- structured RAS, already mapped.} The route-1 source is the \textbf{Russian Financial
Statements Database} (\texttt{irlspbru/RFSD} on HuggingFace; Zenodo DOI \texttt{10.5281/zenodo.14622208};
CC-BY-4.0; cite Bondarkov, Ledenev, and Skougarevskiy (2025), App.~\ref{ch:references}), a firm-level
collection of the whole non-financial universe's unconsolidated RAS statements for
\textbf{2011--2024}. It is distributed as Apache \textbf{Parquet}, \emph{year-partitioned} (\texttt{year=2011}
\ldots{} \texttt{year=2024}), $\sim$7.4~GB in total but $\sim$550~MB for the current year alone --- and a
per-issuer lookup is a column-projected, INN-filtered read of a single partition, not a full scan. Crucially,
the OKUD line codes arrive \emph{already decoded}: a shipped dictionary maps \texttt{line\_2110} to
\texttt{B\_revenue}-style names, with prefixes \texttt{B\_} (balance sheet), \texttt{PL\_} (profit/loss) and
\texttt{CFi\_}/\texttt{CFo\_} (cash flows). So route~1 needs neither a PDF extractor nor a \texttt{vocab\_ras}:
the facts are structured, named, cross-footable (\texttt{line\_1600 = line\_1700}), and keyed by the INN that
MOEX resolves. It refreshes annually (late July / early August; current v2.0.3). A fetched row is a
\texttt{Filing} of kind \texttt{:ras}, and \texttt{facts(f)} maps each OKUD line to a \texttt{Fact} in rubles
(the RFSD structured-row extractor, \S\ref{sys:extraction}), with total assets and total
equity-plus-liabilities cross-footing exactly.

\paragraph{Coverage caveat --- the largest sanctioned firms.} RFSD is built from two public sources ---
Rosstat open data (2012--2018) and the FNS's GIR~BO API (\texttt{bo.nalog.ru}, 2019 onward) --- and inherits
the latter's gaps. Since financial statements for 2018, Russia's \emph{strategic} companies (1{,}133 firms by
end-2023) have been permitted \emph{not} to disclose, and such firms are retrospectively withheld from the
GIR~BO API (Bondarkov, Ledenev, and Skougarevskiy (2025), App.~\ref{ch:references}). The effect is sharp and
datable: an issuer like \textbf{Rosneft}, \textbf{Surgutneftegas} or \textbf{Severstal} carries full RAS data
through 2018 and then \emph{vanishes} from 2019 --- \texttt{facts(f)} returns \emph{no} lines, and the row's
\texttt{filed = 0} flag is exactly how a caller detects it. So RFSD is near-complete for the broad economy
but blind to a specific tail of large, sanctioned issuers in the GIR~BO years; for those names only the
pre-2019 history remains.

\paragraph{The RAS form is the ``taxonomy''.} Because every RAS filer uses the same numbered OKUD lines, the
line codes function as a de-facto taxonomy --- the RAS analogue of \texttt{ifrs-full} concepts or CAS labels,
but numeric and fixed by regulation. Classification is therefore a static code map (balance-sheet and
income-statement lines to the shared statement-type model), not a per-filing label-matching problem. This is
why RFSD is the highest-reuse first route: it slots into the existing structured-fact machinery on the same
axes (revenue, net income, total assets) as every XBRL jurisdiction. Those RAS figures are the
\emph{unconsolidated} legal-entity accounts, so they are \emph{not} comparable to the \emph{consolidated}
IFRS numbers Yahoo / MOEX report for a listed \emph{group} --- there is no market-data oracle for RAS,
unlike the IFRS jurisdictions, so validation rests on internal cross-footing and the quality flags below.

\paragraph{The full statement set --- and the absence of narratives.} RFSD carries the \emph{complete}
statutory RAS \emph{numeric} set, not only the three headline statements: the balance sheet (OKUD 0710001),
the income statement (0710002), the \textbf{statement of changes in equity} (0710003, the \texttt{line\_3xxx}
codes) and the \textbf{cash-flow statement} (0710004), plus the \textbf{report on the targeted use of funds}
(0710006, the \texttt{line\_6xxx} codes --- a non-profit form, usually zero for commercial filers).
\texttt{facts(f; classify = true)} classifies \emph{all five} (\texttt{BalanceSheet} / \texttt{IncomeStatement}
/ \texttt{Equity} / \texttt{CashFlow} / \texttt{TargetedFunds}). What RFSD
does \emph{not} contain is the \textbf{narrative} layer --- the explanatory notes (\rus{пояснения}),
management commentary and the auditor's report --- which are free text in the PDF \rus{годовой отчёт} held by
the e-disclosure agencies (the gated channel, \S\ref{sec:russia-repos}), \emph{not} in the structured
dataset. A \texttt{:ras} filing therefore has \emph{no} text layer: \texttt{narratives(f)} returns an empty
vector, and the notes / MD\&A are reachable only through the (unbuilt) agency-PDF path.

\subsection{Data-quality flags: articulation, totals adjustment, and super-outliers}\label{sec:russia-flags}

RFSD ships a set of per-firm-year \textbf{quality flags} --- computed by its authors --- that
\texttt{EDGAR.jl} carries on every \texttt{:ras} filing and exposes through \texttt{rfsd\_flags(f)}. Three
bear directly on whether a firm-year's numbers can be trusted.

\paragraph{Articulation (\texttt{articulated}).} A statement is \emph{articulated} when its own lines sum to
their subtotals --- the asset lines to total assets, the income lines to net profit, and so on --- within
the official 4{,}000-ruble tolerance (22 identities for the full form, 4 for the simplified one). About 5\%
of filings fail. A non-articulated statement is one whose \emph{own arithmetic does not tie out}: the filer
could not reconcile the form it submitted. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} \emph{keeps} such rows rather than discard
them, because the flag is itself a \textbf{signal} --- notably for \textbf{credit analysis}. A company that
cannot sum its own balance sheet is, other things equal, a worse risk, and an internal scoring model may
well pick \texttt{articulated~=~false} up as a risk factor.

\paragraph{Totals adjustment (\texttt{totals\_adjustment}).} Where a \emph{stated} total was wrong, RFSD's
authors substituted the \emph{calculated} total (again beyond the 4{,}000-ruble threshold). A
\texttt{totals\_adjustment~=~true} row therefore carries figures that were \emph{repaired}, not exactly
as-filed --- a related reliability signal, and a caution when reconciling back to the original document.

\paragraph{Super-outliers (\texttt{outlier}) --- excluded by default.} Separately, the authors
\emph{manually} reviewed the largest firms per industry, the biggest year-on-year swings and the largest
imputed filers, and flagged \textbf{436 firms / 1{,}130 firm-years} as anomalous --- e.g.\ two firms
reporting revenue 8--26$\times$ Gazprom's --- and recommend excluding them (Bondarkov, Ledenev, and
Skougarevskiy (2025), App.~\ref{ch:references}). \texttt{EDGAR.jl} does so \textbf{by default}: a point
\texttt{fetch\_filing} on an \texttt{outlier} row errors, a batch fetch skips it (logging how many), and
population \texttt{discover} filters them in SQL. Pass \texttt{allow\_outliers~=~true} to fetch one anyway.

\paragraph{Granularity --- a company-\emph{year}, not a company.} All three flags are per \textbf{firm-year}:
RFSD has one row per \texttt{(inn, year)} holding that firm's entire statement set, so a company can be
flagged in one year and clean in the next --- and often is (a super-outlier in 2023 may be unremarkable in
2019). Exclusion and the reliability signals therefore attach to a \emph{company-year}, never to the company
as a whole.

\section{Banks --- the CBR channel (route 2)}\label{sec:russia-cbr}

The financial sector that RFSD excludes by law is reached, for \textbf{banks}, through the \textbf{Bank of
Russia}. Each credit institution publishes its statutory reporting as CBR \textbf{Form 0409101} (\rus{оборотная
ведомость} --- a turnover balance by account) and \textbf{0409102} (financial results), served as \textbf{HTML}
by registration number (\texttt{regnum}) and reporting date. This is a wholly different channel from route~1
--- a different source, format (bank chart of accounts, not RFSD rows or XBRL), identity (\texttt{regnum}, not
INN) and statement model --- so it is a distinct \texttt{FilingSource}, \texttt{CBRBankFilings}.

\paragraph{The channel.} \texttt{discover(CBRBankFilings(); regnum \textbar{} name \textbar{} ogrn)} resolves a
bank through the CBR directory (the UniDbQuery XLSX, $\sim$360 currently-licensed banks; \texttt{name} matches
active banks, so ``\rus{Сбербанк}'' $\to$ \texttt{regnum} 1481). \texttt{fetch\_filing} then GETs the Form
HTML --- browser User-Agent, \emph{no} cookie --- as a \texttt{Filing} of kind \texttt{:cbr\_form}; the
reporting date matters (a published month-first returns the full account table, a not-yet-published month an
empty shell), so an unset \texttt{dt} auto-resolves the latest by probing month-firsts back from the current
month.

\paragraph{The bank statement model.} A bank has no revenue or cost of sales, so route~2 uses \emph{one}
presentation statement, \texttt{BankBalanceSheet}. \texttt{facts(f; classify = true)} maps each account (a
\texttt{cbr:<account>} concept, its closing balance in rubles, an instant) to one of $\sim$14 condensed lines
--- \emph{Cash}, \emph{Correspondent accounts}, \emph{Interbank loans and deposits}, \emph{Loans to
customers}, \emph{Securities}, \emph{Fixed \& intangible assets} on the asset side; \emph{Customer current
accounts}, \emph{Customer deposits}, \emph{Issued debt securities} on the liability side; \emph{Share
capital}, \emph{Reserves}, \emph{Retained earnings} in equity --- with the \rus{Актив} / \rus{Пассив}
\texttt{section} dimension fixing the side. The governing invariant is that the turnover balance sheet
\textbf{balances}: $\Sigma\,\rus{Актив} = \Sigma\,\rus{Пассив}$. It holds exactly on every bank tested
(Sberbank 609.55~T~RUB; VTB, Alfa-Bank, Gazprombank and T-Bank all balance), and the headline lines
match the banks' published figures (VTB $\sim$17~T loans / $\sim$19~T customer funds, T-Bank $\sim$1~T).

\paragraph{No narratives; and what is out of scope.} Like the RFSD forms, Form 101/102 are \emph{numeric only}
--- account tables --- so \texttt{narratives(f)} on a \texttt{:cbr\_form} filing returns empty; a bank's
narrative (its annual report and explanatory notes, \rus{пояснительная информация}) lives on the bank's own
disclosure pages, not this channel. Three limits: only \textbf{banks} are reachable (insurers and non-state
pension funds report XBRL to the CBR whose instances are not public); the gross ``paired'' accounts
(inter-branch \texttt{303}, the financial-result \texttt{706}) are classified but not \emph{netted}, so they
dominate the raw totals; and Form 0409102 (the P\&L, a \texttt{BankIncomeStatement}) is a planned extension.

\begin{lstlisting}
using EDGAR, DuckDB
h    = only(discover(CBRBankFilings(); name = "Сбербанк"))  # -> regnum 1481 (active-bank directory match)
f    = fetch_filing(h)                                      # Form 0409101 HTML, latest published month
rows = facts(f; classify = true, labels = true)             # one fact per account, tagged Актив/Пассив
select_statement(rows, "BankBalanceSheet")                  # the condensed bank balance sheet (it balances)
\end{lstlisting}

\chapter{Generic Filing}
\label{ch:generic}

Each chapter before this one adds a \emph{specialised} \texttt{FilingSystem} --- SEC, ESEF, Companies~House,
EDINET, DART, India, China, Hong~Kong. But the world has far more repositories than \texttt{EDGAR.jl} has
adapters: an annual report from Australia, Brazil, Canada or Sweden arrives with no system to receive it. Two
poor options follow --- do not use \texttt{EDGAR.jl} at all, or \emph{force} the document through some existing
jurisdiction's pipeline, whose discovery, vocabulary and quirk-fixes are tuned (\emph{over}-fitted) to a
country that is not this one, and quietly mis-handle it. \textbf{Generic Filing} is the third option: a general
path that, lacking a specialist's advantages, also lacks a specialist's constraints.

\section{Generic is the core with no adapter}

Every specialised \texttt{FilingSystem} in the preceding chapters is really \texttt{Generic} \emph{plus} three
things:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
  \item \textbf{Discovery} --- knowing the repository's API, so \texttt{discover} can \emph{find} a filer's
    filings.
  \item \textbf{A national vocabulary} --- the country's taxonomy label map (\texttt{vocab\_cas},
    \texttt{vocab\_indas}, \ldots), so a native-language line maps to a canonical concept.
  \item \textbf{Quirk handling} --- the overfitted per-jurisdiction fixes (the NSE context-id encoding, Hong
    Kong's dual-currency columns, US month-day dates).
\end{enumerate}
Strip those three away and what remains --- the format-appropriate extractor, the shared taxonomy
vocabularies, and the \texttt{facts}\,/\,\texttt{narratives}\,/\,\texttt{toc} accessors (all of the Common API,
Chapter~\ref{sec:api}) --- is \emph{already} jurisdiction-agnostic. \texttt{Generic} adds no pipeline; it
\textbf{exposes the core directly}. You supply a document you have already obtained from its repository, and
Generic dispatches on its \emph{format}:

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> f = fetch_filing(Generic(), "sydney_airport_2024.pdf")   # you found it; Generic just opens it
julia> facts(f)          # the statements, on ifrs-full / us-gaap concepts
julia> toc(f)            # the outline
julia> narratives(f)     # the prose
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent \texttt{src} is a local path or an \texttt{http(s)} URL; there is no \texttt{discover} step ---
finding the document in the foreign repository is the part a Generic path cannot do for you, and the part you
have already done.

\section{Format, not jurisdiction}

Generic sniffs the document's format from its leading bytes and routes to the extractor that format already
has (Table~\ref{tab:generic-formats}). The pivotal case is tagged \textbf{XBRL}: it is \emph{self-describing}
--- the filing carries its own presentation linkbase --- so an Australian or Swedish IFRS instance classifies
its statements with \emph{zero} national knowledge, exactly as a specialised system would. A tagged foreign
filing is therefore handled about as well by Generic as by a bespoke adapter; the gap is widest for an
untagged, non-English PDF.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Generic dispatches on format, not jurisdiction; each format's extractor classifies \emph{without} a
national vocabulary.}
\label{tab:generic-formats}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}p{0.39\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Format} & \textbf{Generic extractor} & \textbf{Classified by (no national vocab)} \\
\midrule
XBRL / iXBRL & \texttt{extract\_xbrl} (standard-agnostic) & the filing's \textbf{own} presentation linkbase ---
  self-describing \\
English PDF & \texttt{extract\_ifrs\_pdf} (Docling) & the \texttt{ifrs-full} English label map --- IFRS-, not
  country-specific \\
Other PDF & \texttt{extract\_pdf} (Tier-1) & geometric statement detection \\
HTML & \texttt{toc} / \texttt{sections} & the heading / \emph{Item} structure \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\section{A floor, not a ceiling}

What Generic gives up, relative to a specialised path, is exactly the three things above: no \texttt{discover}
(you bring the document), no standardisation of a national-namespace concept to a canonical one, no
quirk-fixes, and no external-benchmark ticker mapping. What it gains is \textbf{universal reach} and
\textbf{robustness}: a pattern-based, IFRS-family extractor is not over-fitted to one country's wording, so it
often survives variation a narrowly tuned path would trip over. And it \textbf{degrades gracefully} --- where
the numbers do not classify (a national-GAAP filing in a language the vocabularies do not cover),
\texttt{toc} still returns the outline, the tables are still parsed, and \texttt{narratives} still returns the
prose. A German annual report opened as \texttt{Generic}, for instance, yields its full bookmark outline from
\texttt{toc} even where the German line labels fall outside the \texttt{ifrs-full} English map. You get
\emph{something}, never nothing, and never a wrong-jurisdiction mangling.

\paragraph{Promotion.} Because a specialised system \emph{is} Generic plus discovery, vocabulary and quirk
handling, a jurisdiction is ``promoted'' out of Generic incrementally: add a \texttt{discover} for its
repository, a label map for its taxonomy, and fixes for its quirks --- each independent, each turning a piece
of the graceful floor into a specialised ceiling. Generic is thus both the fallback for the long tail and the
\emph{starting point} for the next specialised chapter.

\section{Examples from: Australia, Brazil and Canada}


\section{Dealing with Writing Systems}

Only Greece uses a true alphabet on that list. The rest use a fascinating mix of abjads, abugidas, syllabaries, and logograms.

\subsection{Egypt}
Egypt's official language is Arabic, and they use the Arabic alphabet (which, linguistically speaking, is also an abjad).
Just like Hebrew, the Arabic script primarily focuses on consonants. Vowels are usually omitted in everyday writing (like newspapers, books, and street signs) and are only filled in by the reader based on context. When they are written out—like in the Quran, children's books, or grammar texts—they are added as small diacritical marks (called tashkeel) above or below the letters.

\subsection{Greece}
System: Alphabet
How it works: A true alphabet has distinct symbols for both consonants and vowels. The Greek alphabet was actually the world's first true alphabet (derived from the Phoenician system, but they added vowels).

\subsection{Israel}
System: Abjad (Consonantal alphabet)
How it works: Hebrew is written primarily with consonants. Vowels are mostly omitted in everyday writing, though they can be indicated with little dots and dashes called niqqud (usually found in children's books or poetry).


\subsection{Thailand}
System: Abugida (Alphasyllabary) script derived from the Old Khmer script, which ultimately traces back to the ancient South Indian Pallava script. 
How it works: In the Thai script, the base characters are consonants that have an inherent vowel sound automatically attached to them (usually an "a" or "o"). To change the vowel, you add a diacritic mark above, below, before, or after the consonant. The script is written continuously with no spaces between words.

\subsection{Malaysia and Vietnam}
The writing systems of Malaysia and Vietnam are quite similar in their modern forms. Both countries officially use a Latin-based (Romanized) alphabet. However, the foundational rules, accents, and historical evolutions of each are distinct.
\begin{itemize}
  \item Vietnam: Uses the Latin alphabet with an extensive system of diacritics (e.g., ă, â, ê, ơ, ư) to denote vowels and tones. It was developed by 17th-century Portuguese missionaries and replaced a previous writing system (chữ Nôm) that relied on Chinese characters.
  \item Malaysia: Official Script: Rumi, which is the standardized Latin alphabet. It uses the same 26 letters as the English alphabet (with minor spelling agreements to match local pronunciations).Second Script: Jawi, a modified Arabic script used primarily for religious, cultural, and historical heritage purposes.
\end{itemize}
 

\subsection{Cambodia, Laos and Myanmar}
Laos, Cambodia, and Myanmar share a common linguistic heritage rooted in ancient Indian scripts.

\begin{itemize}
  \item Cambodia (Khmer Script): The oldest script in this group. It evolved from the Pallava and ancient Brahmi scripts of Southern India.
  \item Laos (Lao Script): Part of the Tai-Kadai family, the Lao writing system is a direct evolutionary descendant of the Old Khmer script and the ancient Mon scripts.
  \item Myanmar (Burmese Script): While the language is Sino-Tibetan, its writing system was also adapted from Indian roots (like the Pyu and Mon scripts).
\end{itemize}
\section{Local Variations}

\subsection{China: Simplified and Traditional Chinese}
Mandarin and Cantonese do not have an alphabet. Instead, they share a writing system based on Chinese characters, where each character represents a word or meaningful part of a word rather than a sound. While they share the same fundamental writing system, they have major differences:
\begin{itemize}
  \item Written Standard: Mandarin primarily uses Simplified Chinese (used in mainland China), whereas Cantonese predominantly uses Traditional Chinese (used in Hong Kong and Macau).
  \item Spoken Differences: Spoken Mandarin and Cantonese are completely different languages and are not mutually intelligible. A Mandarin speaker cannot understand spoken Cantonese. 
  \item Slang \& Grammar: Cantonese has its own unique spoken grammar and vocabulary that isn't used in Mandarin, and Cantonese speakers often write exactly the way they speak using unique characters.
  \item Phonetics: If a speaker wants to spell out Chinese words using the Roman alphabet (for typing or learning), Mandarin uses Pinyin, while Cantonese has a different system called Jyutping.
\end{itemize}


\subsection{India: Devanagari, Bengali, Gurmukhi and Dravidian Scripts}
 Languages spoken in India do not all use the same alphabet. India has massive linguistic diversity, and languages are written using several distinct scripts. While many share roots in the ancient Brahmi script, they have evolved into unique writing systems.

 The primary scripts used include:
 \begin{itemize}
  \item Devanagari: The script used for Hindi, Sanskrit, Marathi, Nepali, and Konkani.
  \item Dravidian Scripts: Languages in the south have their own distinct scripts, such as Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, and Malayalam.
  \item Other Major Scripts: Bengali, Punjabi (Gurmukhi script), and Odia each use their own distinct writing systems.
  \item Perso-Arabic: Used primarily for Urdu.
  \item Latin: Extensively used for English and sometimes for writing regional languages on digital platforms or in specific localized areas.
 \end{itemize}



\part{Business Reports}



\chapter{Discover: Clients, Suppliers and Competitors}
\label{ch:analysis}

\texttt{EDGAR.jl} is meant to be a \emph{value-added} application, not merely a fetcher of filings. Once a
filing's facts are extracted and classified (\S\ref{sec:api-analysis}), the \emph{same} data --- uniform
across every jurisdiction in the preceding chapters --- supports analysis directly. This chapter frames the
value-added analyses the data makes cheap, with the per-jurisdiction specifics and worked examples. It is
deliberately \emph{light}: each topic here is the shallow end of something that, taken seriously (statistics,
OLAP engines, forecasting, peer benchmarking), deserves its \emph{own} package built \emph{on top of}
\texttt{EDGAR.jl}. The method signatures live in the Common API (\S\ref{sec:api-analysis}); here we give the
concepts, the axes each jurisdiction actually exposes, and how to drive them from Julia.

\section{Dimensional Analysis (drill-down)}
\label{sec:analysis-dimensional}

XBRL is dimensional \emph{by construction}. The XBRL Dimensions specification models a filing's facts as
\textbf{hypercubes}: a \emph{dimension} (axis) ranges over a \emph{domain} of \emph{members}, and a
dimensioned fact is a \emph{cell}. A set of financial statements is therefore already an OLAP cube ---
``revenue'' drilled down by operating segment, by product, by geography is nothing more than fixing a
concept and slicing an axis. \texttt{EDGAR.jl} exposes this directly with \texttt{breakdown} (any axis) and
\texttt{segments} (the segment axis), \textbf{without} a definition linkbase or an external cube: the members
are read from the instance's own contexts, and each member's name from the label linkbase (for \emph{named}
members) or a per-member descriptor text fact (for \emph{generic} ones). Signatures and full options are in
the Common API (\S\ref{sec:api-analysis}).

\paragraph{What each jurisdiction exposes.} Drill-down is only as good as the axes a filer \emph{tags}, and
that varies by regime (Table~\ref{tab:analysis-axes}). Two patterns matter. \textbf{Named members} (SEC,
IFRS/ESEF, EDINET, DART, Companies House) carry a real member concept (\texttt{aapl:AmericasSegmentMember})
whose name resolves from the label linkbase --- for the CJK regimes, pass \texttt{lang="en"} for English
rather than the native names. \textbf{Generic members} (India) carry placeholder members
(\texttt{…Segment01Member}) whose real names live in a separate \texttt{DescriptionOfReportableSegment}
\emph{text} fact per member; \texttt{breakdown} reads that descriptor automatically, so India resolves to
``Retail'', ``Digital Services''\ldots\ all the same. And some regimes simply don't \emph{dimensionally}
tag the segment note at all (ESEF block-tags it), so the cube is sparse where the filer chose prose over tags.

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Analysis axes observed by jurisdiction, and how their members name themselves. The list is
illustrative, not exhaustive --- a filer tags what it discloses.}
\label{tab:analysis-axes}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.16\textwidth}p{0.40\textwidth}p{0.34\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Jurisdiction} & \textbf{Example analysis axes} & \textbf{Member names} \\
\midrule
\textbf{SEC} & \texttt{us-gaap:StatementBusinessSegmentsAxis}, \texttt{srt:ProductOrServiceAxis}, \texttt{srt:ConsolidationItemsAxis} & named; English labels \\
\textbf{ESEF} & \texttt{ifrs-full:SegmentsAxis} \emph{when tagged} --- often the segment note is block-tagged, so absent & named (issuer extension) \\
\textbf{EDINET} & \texttt{jpcrp\_cor:OperatingSegmentsAxis} & named; 日本語 default, \texttt{lang="en"} for English \\
\textbf{DART} & \texttt{ifrs-full:SegmentsAxis} & named; 한국어 default, \texttt{lang="en"} for English \\
\textbf{India} & \texttt{in-bse-fin:ReportableSegmentsAxis} (and the \texttt{in-capmkt} equivalent) & \emph{generic} members, named automatically from their \texttt{DescriptionOfReportableSegment} text fact \\
\textbf{Companies House} & \texttt{uk-core:MaturitiesOrExpirationPeriodsDimension}, \texttt{uk-bus} officer/metadata axes; business segments rare & named; all FRC-module labels \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Worked example.} Apple's latest 10-K, revenue drilled down two ways --- by operating segment and
by product:

\begin{lstlisting}
julia> f = fetch_filing(320193, "0000320193-25-000079");
julia> segments(f, "us-gaap:RevenueFromContractWithCustomerExcludingAssessedTax")
        # Americas $178B · Europe $111B · Greater China $64B · Rest of Asia Pacific $34B · Japan $29B
julia> breakdown(f, "us-gaap:RevenueFromContractWithCustomerExcludingAssessedTax";
                 axis = "srt:ProductOrServiceAxis")
        # iPhone $210B · Services $109B · Wearables $36B · Mac $34B · iPad $28B
\end{lstlisting}

\noindent The segment figures sum to Apple's total revenue --- the cube is internally consistent, which is
itself checkable (see below). For a Japanese or Korean filer the same two calls work; add \texttt{lang="en"}
to read ``Automotive'' / ``DRAM'' rather than \jpcjk{自動車}{} / DRAM.

\paragraph{Siblings (to grow).} Dimensional drill-down is one analysis the data supports; others share the
same ``pure function of a \texttt{Filing}'' shape and will join this chapter. The natural next axis is
\emph{cross-filer comparison} --- the same standardized concept across peers or across time. (Checking that
a filing's numbers are self-consistent --- \texttt{check\_calculations} and the accounting-rule checks --- is
a distinct concern, \emph{validation} rather than analysis; it lives in \S\ref{sec:validation-rules}.) Each
of these stays framed here and deep in its own package.

\appendix

\chapter{Glossary}
\label{sec:glossary}

SEC filings and their structured data come with their own vocabulary.
The terms that matter here:

\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.4em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
  \item[CIK] \emph{Central Index Key}, the SEC's unique ID for a filer,
    zero-padded to 10 digits (Apple $\to$ \texttt{0000320193}). Functions accept it
    as an integer or a string, with or without leading zeros (\texttt{320193}
    $\equiv$ \texttt{0000320193}). Look one up from a ticker or company name with
    \texttt{cik}.
  \item[Filing] a document submitted to the SEC, identified by an
    \textbf{accession number} and a \textbf{form type} (\texttt{10-K} annual
    report, \texttt{10-Q} quarterly, \texttt{8-K} current report, \dots). Get a filer's filings with \texttt{filings\_by\_cik}.
  \item[XBRL] \emph{eXtensible Business Reporting Language}, the machine-readable format companies tag their financial statements in; it is what makes the numbers queryable rather than locked inside a document. A pure XBRL filing is an XML document whose primary purpose is to represent structured facts,
  \begin{lstlisting}
<us-gaap:Assets contextRef="CurrentYear" unitRef="USD">
    1000000
</us-gaap:Assets>
\end{lstlisting}
A browser won't render this as a nice financial statement. It's intended for software.
\item[iXBRL] \emph{Inline-XBRL}: those same tags embedded directly inside the filing's human-readable HTML, so one document is both readable and
machine-parseable. The SEC has required it for financial statements since
around 2019. An iXBRL filing is an HTML document that looks like a normal report,
\begin{lstlisting}
<p>
Total assets were
<ix:nonFraction
    name="us-gaap:Assets"
    contextRef="CurrentYear"
    unitRef="USD">
    1000000
</ix:nonFraction>.
</p>
\end{lstlisting}
Historically, iXBRL was defined on top of XHTML (XML-compliant HTML), although modern tools often treat it as HTML with XML namespaces.
  \item[Fact] a single reported number: one \emph{concept}, for one
    \emph{period}, in one \emph{unit} (e.g.\ ``net income for FY2023 was
    96{,}995{,}000{,}000 USD''). \texttt{company\_facts} returns every fact a
    company has reported.
  \item[Concept] the \emph{name} of a reported line item, drawn from a taxonomy
    (e.g.\ \texttt{NetIncomeLoss}, \texttt{Assets}, \texttt{Revenues}) --- ``what is
    being measured''. \texttt{company\_concept} returns one concept over time.
  \item[Taxonomy] a dictionary that defines concepts. The big one is
    \textbf{\texttt{us-gaap}} (US accounting standards); \textbf{\texttt{dei}}
    (\emph{Document and Entity Information}) holds metadata about the filing
    itself; \textbf{\texttt{srt}} holds shared elements; \textbf{\texttt{ifrs-full}}
    is for filers reporting under IFRS; and each company has its own
    \textbf{extension} namespace (e.g.\ \texttt{aapl} for Apple-specific concepts).
  \item[Frame] a cross-section: one concept, one unit, one period, across
    \emph{every} filer that reported it. \texttt{xbrl\_frames} returns a frame.
  \item[Unit] the unit of measure of a fact: \texttt{USD}, \texttt{shares},
    \texttt{USD/shares} (per-share), \texttt{pure} (a ratio), \dots
  \item[Period] the time a fact covers, and how it is written. \textbf{Frames}
    use \emph{calendar} periods: \texttt{CY} + year, as in \texttt{CY2022} (a full
    calendar year), \texttt{CY2022Q4} (a quarter), or \texttt{CY2022Q4I} (a single
    instant --- note the trailing \texttt{I}). An \textbf{instant} is a point in
    time (balance-sheet items such as Assets, at quarter-end); a
    \textbf{duration} is a span (income-statement items such as Revenue). By
    contrast, \textbf{\texttt{FY}} in a company's own facts is its \emph{fiscal}
    year, which need not match the calendar (Apple's \texttt{FY2023} ended in
    September 2023).
\end{description}
